The **Mikoyan MiG-29** ([Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language): Микоян МиГ-29; [NATO reporting name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO_reporting_name): **Fulcrum**) is a [twin-engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twinjet) [fighter aircraft](10929.md) designed in the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union). Developed by the [Mikoyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan) [design bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Design_bureau) as an [air superiority fighter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_superiority_fighter) during the 1970s, the MiG-29, along with the larger [Sukhoi Su-27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi_Su-27), was developed to counter U.S. fighters such as the [McDonnell Douglas F-15 Eagle](11715.md) and the [General Dynamics F-16 Fighting Falcon](11642.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordonDavison20059-2] The MiG-29 entered service with the [Soviet Air Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Air_Forces) in 1983.

While originally oriented towards combat against any enemy aircraft, many MiG-29s have been furnished as [multirole fighters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multirole_combat_aircraft) capable of performing a number of different operations, and are commonly outfitted to use a range of [air-to-surface armaments](1544116.md) and [precision munitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precision-guided_munition). The MiG-29 has been manufactured in several major variants, including the multirole [Mikoyan MiG-29M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan_MiG-29M) and the navalised [Mikoyan MiG-29K](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan_MiG-29K); the most advanced member of the family to date is the [Mikoyan MiG-35](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan_MiG-35). Later models frequently feature improved engines, [glass cockpits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glass_cockpit) with [HOTAS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HOTAS) ("hands-on-throttle-and-stick")-compatible flight controls, modern radar and [infrared search and track](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrared_search_and_track) (IRST) sensors, and considerably increased fuel capacity; some aircraft have also been equipped for [aerial refueling](237949.md).

Following the dissolution of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union), the militaries of multiple [ex-Soviet republics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_states) have continued to operate the MiG-29, the largest of them being the [Russian Aerospace Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Aerospace_Forces). The Russian Aerospace Forces wanted to upgrade its existing fleet to the modernised MiG-29SMT configuration, but financial difficulties have limited deliveries. The MiG-29 has also been a popular export aircraft; more than 30 nations either operate or have operated the aircraft. As of 2026 an estimated 728 MiG-29/35 variants remain in service worldwide, representing the sixth largest fleet and 5% of active combat aircraft.[^cite_note-i536-3]

## Development

### Origins

In the mid-1960s, the [United States Air Force](32090.md) (USAF) encountered difficulties over the skies of Vietnam. Supersonic fighter bombers that had been optimized for low altitude bombing, like the [F-105 Thunderchief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-105_Thunderchief), were found to be vulnerable to older [MiG-17s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan-Gurevich_MiG-17) and more advanced MiGs which were much more maneuverable.[^cite_note-4] In order to regain limited air superiority, the US refocused on air combat using the [F-4 Phantom](11759.md) multirole fighter, while the Soviet Union developed the [MiG-23](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MiG-23) in response. Towards the end of the 1960s, the USAF started the "F-X" program to produce a fighter dedicated to air superiority, which led to the [McDonnell Douglas F-15 Eagle](11715.md) being ordered for production in late 1969.[^cite_note-Jenkins_p9-11-5]

At the height of the [Cold War](325329.md), a Soviet response was necessary to avoid the possibility of the Americans gaining a serious technological advantage over the Soviets, thus the development of a new air superiority fighter became a priority.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordonDavison20059-2] In 1969, the Soviet General Staff issued a requirement for a *Perspektivnyy Frontovoy Istrebitel* (*PFI*, roughly "Advanced Frontline Fighter").[^cite_note-Spick_488_513-6] Specifications were extremely ambitious, calling for long range, good short-field performance (including the ability to use austere runways), excellent agility, Mach 2+ speed, and heavy armament. The Russian aerodynamics institute [TsAGI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TsAGI) worked in collaboration with the [Sukhoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi) design bureau on the aircraft's aerodynamics.[^cite_note-Spick_488_513-6]



A [Soviet Air Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Air_Forces) MiG-29 parked after a display flight at the [Abbotsford Air Show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbotsford_Air_Show), 1989

By 1971, however, Soviet studies determined the need for different types of fighters. The PFI program was supplemented with the *Perspektivnyy Lyogkiy Frontovoy Istrebitel* (*LPFI*, or "Advanced Lightweight Tactical Fighter") program; the Soviet fighter force was planned to be approximately 33% PFI and 67% LPFI.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordonDavison20058–9-7] PFI and LPFI paralleled the USAF's decision that created the "Lightweight Fighter" program and the [General Dynamics F-16 Fighting Falcon](11642.md) and [Northrop YF-17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northrop_YF-17).[^cite_note-8] The PFI fighter was assigned to Sukhoi, resulting in the [Sukhoi Su-27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi_Su-27), while the lightweight fighter went to Mikoyan. Detailed design work on the resultant Mikoyan *Product 9*, designated *MiG-29A*, began in 1974, with the first flight taking place on 6 October 1977. The pre-production [aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft) was first spotted by United States [reconnaissance satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconnaissance_satellite) in November of that year; it was dubbed *Ram-L* because it was observed at the Zhukovsky flight test center near the town of [Ramenskoye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramenskoye,_Moscow_Oblast).[^cite_note-wapj36_p110-1-9][^cite_note-jawa93_p238-10]

The workload split between TPFI and LPFI became more apparent as the MiG-29 filtered into frontline service with the [Soviet Air Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Air_Forces) (Russian: *Voenno-Vozdushnye Sily* \[VVS]) in the mid-1980s. While the heavy, long range [Su-27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Su-27) was tasked with the more exotic and dangerous role of deep air-to-air sweeps of NATO high-value assets, the smaller MiG-29 directly replaced the [MiG-23](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MiG-23) in the frontal aviation role.

### Introduction and improvements

In the West, the new fighter was given the [NATO reporting name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO_reporting_name) "Fulcrum-A" because the pre-production MiG-29A, which should have logically received this designation, remained unknown in the West at that time. The Soviet Union did not assign official names to most of its aircraft, although nicknames were common. Unusually, some Soviet pilots found the MiG-29's NATO reporting name, "[Fulcrum](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/fulcrum)", to be a flattering description of the aircraft's intended purpose, and it is sometimes unofficially used in Russian service.[^cite_note-zuyev_fulcrum-11]

The MiG-29 was widely exported in downgraded versions, known as *MiG-29 9-12A* for [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact) and *MiG-29 9-12B* for non-Warsaw Pact nations,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon200665-12] with less capable [avionics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avionics) and no capability for delivering [nuclear weapons](21785.md).

In the 1980s, [Mikoyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan) developed the improved MiG-29S to use longer range [R-27E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vympel_R-27) air-to-air missiles. It added a dorsal 'hump' to the upper [fuselage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuselage) to house a [jamming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_jamming) system and some additional fuel capacity. The weapons load was increased to 4,000 kg (8,800 lb) with airframe strengthening. These features were included in new-built fighters and upgrades to older MiG-29s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordonDavison200527–29-13][^cite_note-14]



A Russian Air Force MiG-29UB trainer landing

Refined versions of the MiG-29 with improved avionics were fielded by the Soviet Union, but Mikoyan's multirole variants, including a [carrier-based](2219.md) version designated [MiG-29K](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan_MiG-29K), were never produced in large numbers. Development of the MiG-29K version for use in [aircraft carriers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft_carriers), was suspended for over a decade before being resumed. The type went into service with the [Indian Navy](604522.md)'s [INS *Vikramaditya*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INS_Vikramaditya),[^cite_note-Navy-15] [INS *Vikrant*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INS_Vikrant_\(2013\)),[^cite_note-\:2-16] and [Russian Navy](383258.md)'s [*Kuznetsov* class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuznetsov-class_aircraft_carrier) aircraft carriers.[^cite_note-17] Mikoyan also developed improved versions [MiG-29M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan_MiG-29M) and [MiG-29SMT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan_MiG-29M#Variants).[^cite_note-18][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordonDavison200530,_34-19]



[Russian Naval Aviation](5698051.md)'s various MiG-29s stationed at [Kubinka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kubinka_\(air_base\)) alongside the newer and heavier Su-30SM

There have been several upgrade programmes conducted for the MiG-29. Common upgrades include the adoption of standard-compatible avionics, service life extensions to 4,000 flight hours, safety enhancements, greater combat capabilities and reliability.

### Replacement

On 11 December 2013, Russian deputy prime minister [Dmitry Rogozin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Rogozin) revealed that Russia was planning to build a new fighter to replace the MiG-29. The [Sukhoi Su-27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi_Su-27) and its derivatives were to be replaced by the [Sukhoi Su-57](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi_Su-57), but a different design was needed to directly replace the lighter MiGs. A previous attempt to develop a MiG-29 replacement, the [MiG 1.44](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MiG_1.44) demonstrator, failed in the 1990s. The concept came up again in 2001 with interest from India, but they later opted for a variant of the Su-57. Air Force commanders have hinted at the possibility of a single-engine airframe that uses the Su-57's engine, radar, and weapons primarily for Russian service.[^cite_note-20] This has since been revealed to be the [Sukhoi Su-75 Checkmate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi_Su-75_Checkmate). Meanwhile, in Russian service, the MiG-29 has been largely replaced by the [Sukhoi Su-30SM](552808.md), representing a loss in market share of the MiG-29 series to the Sukhoi heavier platforms.

## Design



MiG-29UB of the [Swifts aerobatic team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swifts_\(aerobatic_team\))

Sharing its origins in the original PFI requirements issued by [TsAGI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TsAGI), the MiG-29 has broad aerodynamic similarities to the [Sukhoi Su-27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi_Su-27), but with some notable differences. The MiG-29 has a mid-mounted [swept wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swept_wing) with blended [leading-edge root extensions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leading_edge_extension) (LERXs) swept at around 40°; there are swept tailplanes and two vertical fins, mounted on booms outboard of the engines. Automatic [slats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leading_edge_slats) are mounted on the [leading edges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leading_edge) of the wings; they are four-segment on early models and five-segment on some later variants. On the [trailing edge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trailing_edge), there are maneuvering flaps and wingtip [ailerons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aileron).[^cite_note-books.google.com.eg-21]

The MiG-29 has hydraulic controls and a SAU-451 three-axis autopilot but, unlike the Su-27, no fly-by-wire control system. Nonetheless, it is very agile, with excellent instantaneous and sustained turn performance, [high-alpha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angle_of_attack) capability, and a general resistance to spins. The airframe consists primarily of aluminum with some composite materials,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon200655,_57-22] and is stressed for up to 9 g (88 m/s²) maneuvers. The controls have "soft" limiters to prevent the pilot from exceeding g and alpha limits, but the limiters can be disabled manually.[^cite_note-books.google.com.eg-21]

### Powerplant, performance and range

Main article: [Klimov RD-33](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klimov_RD-33)



[RD-33](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klimov_RD-33) on display at the [Luftwaffenmuseum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luftwaffenmuseum) of the Bundeswehr



A [Bangladesh Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Air_Force) MiG-29 inflight with its [Klimov RD-33](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klimov_RD-33) [turbofan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turbofan) engines on full [afterburner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afterburner)&#x20;

The MiG-29 has two widely spaced [Klimov RD-33](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klimov_RD-33) [turbofan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turbofan) engines, each rated at 50 kilonewtons (11,200 lbf) dry and 81.3 kilonewtons (18,300 lbf) in [afterburner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afterburner_\(engine\)). The space between the engines generates [lift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lift_\(force\)), thereby reducing effective [wing loading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wing_loading), hence improving maneuverability. The engines are fed through [intake ramps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intake_ramp) fitted under the [leading-edge extensions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leading_edge_extension) (LERXs), which have variable ramps to allow high-[Mach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mach_number) speeds. Due to their relatively short combustor, the engines produce noticeably heavier smoke than their contemporaries. As an adaptation to rough-field operations, the main air inlet can be closed completely and the auxiliary air inlet on the upper fuselage can be used for takeoff, landing and low-altitude flying, preventing [ingestion of ground debris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_object_damage). Thereby the engines receive air through louvers on the LERXs which open automatically when intakes are closed. However the latest variant of the family, the [MiG-35](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan_MiG-35), eliminated these dorsal louvers, and adopted the mesh screens design in the main intakes, similar to those fitted to the Su-27.[^cite_note-23]

The MiG-29 has a ferry range of 1,500 km (930 mi) without external fuel tanks, and 2,100 km (1,300 mi) with external tanks.[^cite_note-airforce-technology.com-24] The internal fuel capacity of the original MiG-29 (Fulcrum-A) is 4,300 L (950 imp gal; 1,100 US gal) distributed between six internal [fuel tanks](1071636.md), four in the fuselage and one in each wing.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon2006341-25] The internal fuel capacity of the MiG-29 (Fulcrum-C) is 4,540 L (1,000 imp gal; 1,200 US gal) due to a larger #1 fuselage tank.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon2006341-25] For longer flights, this can be supplemented by a 1,500 L (330 imp gal; 400 US gal) centreline [drop tank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drop_tank) on the Fulcrum-A and two 1,150 L (250 imp gal; 300 US gal) underwing drop tanks on later production batches. In addition, newer models have been fitted with port-side [inflight refueling](237949.md) probes, allowing much longer flight times by using a [probe-and-drogue system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Probe_and_drogue).[^cite_note-airforce-technology.com-24]

### Cockpit



MiG-29 cockpit, 1995

The cockpit features a conventional [centre stick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_stick) and left hand throttle controls. The pilot sits in a [Zvezda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NPP_Zvezda) [K-36DM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NPP_Zvezda_K-36) [ejection seat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ejection_seat).

The cockpit has conventional dials, with a [head-up display](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head-up_display) (HUD) and a Shchel-3UM [helmet mounted display](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helmet_mounted_display), but no [HOTAS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HOTAS) capability. Emphasis seems to have been placed on making the cockpit similar to the earlier MiG-23 and other Soviet aircraft for ease of conversion, rather than on [ergonomics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ergonomics). Nonetheless, the MiG-29 does have substantially better visibility than most previous Soviet jet fighters, thanks to a high-mounted [bubble canopy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bubble_canopy). Upgraded models introduce "[glass cockpits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glass_cockpit)" with modern [liquid-crystal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LCD) (LCD) [multi-function displays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-function_display) (MFDs) and true HOTAS.

### Sensors

The baseline MiG-29 9.12 has a [Phazotron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phazotron) RLPK-29 [radar](25676.md) [fire control system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_control_system) which includes the N019 *Sapfir 29* [look-down/shoot-down](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Look-down/shoot-down) coherent [pulse-Doppler radar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulse-Doppler_radar) and the Ts100.02-02 digital computer.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon200658-26]



MiG-29 nose showing radome and S-31E2 KOLS [IRST](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IRST)

The N019 radar was not a new design, but rather a development of the Sapfir-23ML architecture used on the MiG-23ML. During the initial design specification period in the mid-1970s, Phazotron NIIR was tasked with producing a modern radar for the MiG-29. To speed development, Phazotron based its new design on work undertaken by NPO Istok on the experimental "*Soyuz*" radar program. Accordingly, the N019 was originally intended to have a flat [planar array](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planar_array_radar) antenna and full [digital signal processing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_signal_processing), for a detection and tracking range of at least 100 km (62 mi) against a fighter-sized target. Prototype testing revealed this could not be attained in the required timeframe and still fit within the MiG-29's nose. Rather than design a new radar, Phazotron reverted to a version of the Sapfir-23ML's twisted-polarization [cassegrain antenna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassegrain_antenna) and traditional analog signal processors, coupled with a new NII Argon-designed Ts100 digital computer to save time and cost. This produced a working radar system, but inherited the weak points of the earlier design, plaguing the MiG-29's ability to detect and track airborne targets at ranges available with the [R-27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vympel_R-27) and [R-77](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-77) missiles.



MiG-29UB on display, showing gunport

The N019 was further compromised by Phazotron designer [Adolf Tolkachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolf_Tolkachev)'s betrayal of the radar to the [CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA), for which he was executed in 1986. In response to all of these problems, the Soviets hastily developed a modified N019M *Topaz* radar for the upgraded *MiG-29S* aircraft. However, *VVS* was reportedly still not satisfied with the performance of the system and demanded another upgrade. The latest upgraded aircraft, MiG-29M, offered the N010 [*Zhuk-M*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhuk_radar), which has a planar array antenna rather than a dish, improving range, and a much superior processing ability, with multiple-target engagement capability and compatibility with the Vympel R-77 (or RVV-AE).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon2006431-27]

### Armament



[Bangladesh Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Air_Force) MiG-29 showing its full underbelly. Note the six underwing [pylons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardpoint) carrying [R-27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vympel_R-27) and [R-73](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vympel_R-73) [air-to-air missiles](320568.md). The centerline fuel tank is seen with an APU exhaust duct.

Armament for the MiG-29 includes a single [GSh-30-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gryazev-Shipunov_GSh-30-1) 30 mm (1.18 in) cannon in the port wing root. This originally had a 150-round magazine, which was reduced to 100 rounds in later variants, which only allows a few seconds of firing before running out of ammo. Original production MiG-29 aircraft cannot fire the cannon when carrying a centerline fuel tank as it blocks the shell ejection port. This was corrected in the MiG-29S and later versions.

Three [pylons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardpoint) are provided under each wing (four in some variants), for a total of six (or eight). The inboard pylons can carry either a 1,150 L (250 imp gal; 300 US gal) fuel tank, one *Vympel* [R-27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vympel_R-27) (AA-10 "Alamo") medium-range air-to-air missile, or unguided bombs or rockets. Some Soviet aircraft could carry a single nuclear bomb on the port inboard station. The outer pylons usually carry [R-73](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vympel_R-73) (AA-11 "Archer") dogfight air to air missiles, although some users still retain the older [R-60](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molniya_R-60) (AA-8 "Aphid"). A single 1,500 L (330 imp gal; 400 US gal) tank can be fitted to the centerline, between the engines.

The US has supplied [AGM-88 HARM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AGM-88_HARM) missiles to Ukraine. It appears that they are fired from MiG-29s. It was only disclosed after Russian forces showed footage of a tail fin from one of these missiles.[^cite_note-28] U.S. Under Secretary of Defense for Policy [Colin Kahl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Kahl) has said this: "I would just point to two things. One, you know, a lot was made about the MiG-29 issue several months ago, not very much has been noticed about the sheer amount of spare parts and other things that we've done to help them actually put more of their own MiG-29s in the air and keep those that are in the air flying for a longer period of time. And then also, in recent PDA \[Presidential Drawdown Authority] packages we've included a number of anti-radiation missiles that can be fired off of Ukrainian aircraft. They can have effects on Russian radars and other things."[^cite_note-29] Soviet era aircraft don't have the computer architecture to accept NATO standard weapons. The interface would be difficult; however with a "crude modification", such as an e-tablet, it would be possible.[^cite_note-30]

## Operational history

While the MiG-29's true capabilities could only be estimated from the time it first appeared in 1977 until the mid-1980s, a combination of persistent intelligence and increasing access afforded by the Soviet foreign sales effort allowed a true appreciation of its capabilities. Early MiG-29s were very agile aircraft, capable of rivaling the performance of contemporary [F-18](45998.md) and [F-16](11642.md) aircraft. However, their relatively low fuel capacity relegated them to short-range air defense missions. Lacking HOTAS and an inter-aircraft data link, and requiring a very intensive "heads-down" approach to operating cockpit controls, the early MiG-29 denied pilots the kind of [situational awareness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Situational_awareness) routinely enjoyed by pilots operating comparable US aircraft. Analysts and Western pilots who flew examples of the MiG-29 thought this likely prevented even very good pilots from harnessing the plane's full combat capability. Later MiG-29s were upgraded to improve their capabilities.[^cite_note-31] The Soviet Union exported MiG-29s to several countries. Because 4th-generation fighter aircraft require the pilots to have extensive training, air-defense infrastructure, and constant maintenance and upgrades, MiG-29s have had mixed operational history with different air forces.[^cite_note-Strat-32]

### Soviet Union and successor states

#### Soviet service

The MiG-29 was first publicly seen in the West when the Soviet Union displayed the aircraft in [Finland](10577.md) on 2 July 1986. Two MiG-29s were also displayed at the [Farnborough Airshow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farnborough_Airshow) in Britain in September 1988. The following year, the aircraft conducted flying displays at the 1989 [Paris Air Show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Air_Show) where it was involved in a non-fatal crash during the first weekend of the show.[^cite_note-33] The Paris Air Show display was only the second display of Soviet fighters at an international air show since the 1930s. Western observers were impressed by its apparent capability and exceptional agility.

The only claimed combat service of the MiG-29 in Soviet service was in 1987 and 1988, where they were deployed the [115th GvIAP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/115th_Guards_Fighter_Aviation_Regiment?action=edit\&redlink=1) to [Termez Airfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Termez_Airport) in [Turkmenistan](198149.md) in 1987 to cover for Soviet forces in Afghanistan, especially [Kabul](16826.md). This apparently proved effective as - sources disagree about whether it was in August or September - four [SU-22M3s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi_Su-17) of the [DRAAF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Air_Force_\(1978%E2%80%931992\)) defected and attempted to bomb the capital but were all shot down within minutes of each other by the Fulcrums,[^cite_note-\:8-34] though this incident is heavily disputed. The Fulcrums of the 115th GvIAP were however, used to overfly and interrupt two unidentified Afghans who had crossed the Soviet border and were constructing a hut on an islet in the Pyandzh River.[^cite_note-35] Following the disintegration of the Soviet Union, most of the MiG-29s entered service with the newly formed [Russian Air Force](1526308.md).

#### Russia



The sketch of the MiG-29 performing the [super maneuver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supermaneuverability), the [Pugachev's Cobra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pugachev's_Cobra)

In July 1993, two MiG-29s of the Russian Air Force collided in mid-air and crashed away from the public at the [Royal International Air Tattoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_International_Air_Tattoo). No one on the ground sustained any serious injuries, and the two pilots [ejected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ejection_seat) and landed safely.[^cite_note-36]



MiG-29SMT at the 2011 MAKS

The Russian Air Force grounded all its MiG-29s following a crash in [Siberia](29639.md) on 17 October 2008. Following a second crash with a MiG-29 in east Siberia in December 2008,[^cite_note-37] Russian officials admitted that most MiG-29 fighters in the Russian Air Force were incapable of performing combat duties due to poor maintenance. The age of the aircraft was also an important factor as about 70% of the MiGs were considered to be too old to take to the skies. The Russian MiG-29s had not received updates since the collapse of the Soviet Union. On 4 February 2009, the Russian Air Force resumed flights with the MiG-29. However, in March 2009, 91 MiG-29s of the Russian Air Force required repair after inspections due to corrosion; approximately 100 MiGs were cleared to continue flying at the time.[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39] The Russian Aerospace Forces started an update of its early MiG-29s to the more current MiG-29SMT standard,[^cite_note-airforce-technology.com-24] but financial difficulties prevented delivery of more than three MiG-29 SMT upgrade to the Russian Aerospace Forces. Instead, the 35 MiG-29SMT/UBTs rejected by Algeria were bought by the Russian Aerospace Forces.[^cite_note-40] Russia placed an order for 16 new-build MiG-29SMTs on 15 April 2014, with delivery expected by 2017.[^cite_note-41]

On 4 June 2015, a MiG-29 crashed during training in [Astrakhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrakhan).[^cite_note-42] A month later, another MiG-29 crashed near the village of Kushchevskaya in the Krasnodar region with the pilot safely ejecting.[^cite_note-43] A series of accidents in the Russian Aerospace Forces that happened in 2015 were caused mostly by overall increase of flights and training.[^cite_note-44]

##### 2008 Georgian drone shootdown

On 20 April 2008, Georgian officials claimed a Russian MiG-29 [shot down a Georgian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Georgian_spy_plane_shootdowns) [Hermes 450](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermes_450) [unmanned aerial vehicle](58900.md) and provided video footage from the ill-fated drone showing an apparent MiG-29 launching an air-to-air missile at it. Russia denies that the aircraft was theirs and says they did not have any pilots in the air that day. [Abkhazia](18933375.md)'s administration claimed its own forces shot down the drone with an [L-39](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aero_L-39_Albatros) aircraft "because it was violating Abkhaz airspace and breaching ceasefire agreements."[^cite_note-45] UN investigation concluded that the video was authentic and that the drone was shot down by a Russian MiG-29 or Su-27 using an R-73 heat seeking missile.[^cite_note-46]

##### Russian intervention in Syria

Following the deployment of the [Kuznetsov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_aircraft_carrier_Admiral_Kuznetsov) aircraft carrier off the Syrian Coast, MiG-29Ks from the carrier air wing took part in operations against insurgent forces in the country. On the 13th of November 2016, one of these MiG-29Ks crashed after running out of fuel while waiting for repair on the landing systems aboard the ship. The pilot was rescued by a Russian helicopter after ejecting.[^cite_note-47]

During the first half of September 2017, the Russian Aerospace Forces deployed some MiG-29SMT multirole combat aircraft to [Khmeimim Airbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmeimim_Air_Base), near Latakia, in western Syria, becoming the first time the modernized version of the baseline Fulcrum jet was deployed to take part in the Syrian Air War.[^cite_note-48] The MiG-29SMT were involved in bombing missions and secondary strategic bombers escort duties of Russian and Syrian aircraft.[^cite_note-49]

##### Russian invasion of Ukraine

On 16 July 2014, a Ukrainian Su-25 was shot down, with Ukrainian officials stating that a Russian MiG-29 shot it down using a [R-27T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-27_\(air-to-air_missile\)) missile.[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51] Russia denied these allegations.[^cite_note-52]

Following the [2022 Russian Invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md), Russia has not used MiG-29s in a large scale, with little evidence of their use in publicly available information. Ukrainian forces claim to have destroyed a MiG-29 at Kacha Airfield in [Crimea](163045.md) on the 4th of December 2025 using a long range drone launched by the "Ghosts" Special Forces Unit. Images released by Ukrainian forces indicate that it was a MiG-29K, though it is unclear if it was a decoy or even if it was operational.[^cite_note-53]

#### Kazakhstan

Kazakhstan inherited a large amount of the Soviet Union's stockpiles, including at least 12 MiG-29As (Project 9.12s) and two MiG-29UBs of the 715th Fighter Aviation Regiment based at Loogovaya Airbase. In 1995, the newly re-organized [Kazak Air Defense Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Air_Defense_Forces) ordered 22 more Fulcrums from Russia, including at least 18 single seat fighters (Project 9.12s) and four trainers.[^cite_note-54] Between 1995 and 1996, at least two airframes were reportedly sold onto Yemen.[^cite_note-HSOY_2_14-55] MiG-29s were retired from active Kazakh service in 2023, when around 23 were still believed to be active[^cite_note-World_Air_Forces_2023-56] and have since been offered for sale by the government. A number of sources claimed that these aircraft, alongside other former Soviet types such as the MiG-31, were sold to the United States either to be used as an aggressor type or to be transferred to Ukraine, with the Kazakh government denying this and clarifying that the auction was only open to local entities.[^cite_note-\:10-57][^cite_note-\:11-58][^cite_note-\:12-59][^cite_note-\:13-60]

#### Turkmenistan

[Turkmenistan](198149.md) inherited a number of MiG-29s from the Soviet Union, especially those based within its borders with these aircraft being originally from the 1521st Detachment at the Maryy-1 Airbase until their disbandment in 1993. They were then re-organized into the 67th Composite Air Regiment of the [Turkmen Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkmen_Air_Force) at the Maryy-2 Airbase. A total of 24 airframes are believed to have been inherited: 10 MiG-29As (9.12 airframes), 12 MiG-29Cs (9.13 airframes) and two MiG-29UBs.[^cite_note-61]

#### Ukraine

See also: [List of aircraft losses during the Russo-Ukrainian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_aircraft_losses_during_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War)



A [Ukrainian](31750.md) MiG-29, 2018

Ukraine inherited up to 240 from the Soviet Union in 1991, but over the years several were withdrawn from service, sold to other countries, while others were handed over to museums. Until 2011, approximately 70−80 aircraft were used by the 9th, 40th, 114th, and 204th Brigades, though fewer than 20 were fully mission capable on average.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECooperFontanellazSipos202430−31-62] In 2019, it was estimated that Ukraine had only 37 combat ready MiG-29s.[^cite_note-63] After the Russian invasion in February 2022, three [Azerbaijani Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijani_Air_Force) MiG-29s undergoing overhauls were transferred to the [Ukrainian Air Force](1135769.md), bolstering the total of aircraft operated by the 40th and 204th Tactical Aviation Brigades from around 55 to 58.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECooperFontanellazSipos202431-64]

##### Russian invasions of Ukraine

In April 2014, during the [Russian invasion of Crimea](42085878.md), 45 Ukrainian Air Force MiG-29s and 4 L-39 combat trainers were reportedly captured by Russian forces at [Belbek air base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sevastopol_International_Airport). Most of the planes appeared to be in inoperable condition. In May, Russian troops dismantled them and shipped them back to Ukraine. On 4 August 2014, the Ukrainian government stated that a number of them had been put back into service to fight in the war in the east of the country.[^cite_note-65]

During the initial days of the [war in Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Donbas_\(2014%E2%80%932022\)) in April 2014, the Ukrainian Air Force deployed some jet fighters over the Donetsk region to perform combat air patrols and show of force flights. Probably due to the limited number of jet fighters available, a MiG-29 belonging to the [Ukrainian Falcons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Falcons) display team was spotted armed with a full air-to-air load and performing a low altitude fly by.[^cite_note-66] In the evening of 7 August 2014, a Ukrainian Air Force MiG-29MU1, bort number 02 Blue, was shot down by an antiaircraft missile fired by pro-Russian rebels near the town of Yenakievo, and exploded in midair. The pilot ejected safely.[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69] On 17 August 2014, another Ukrainian Air Force MiG-29, bort number 53 White, tasked with air to ground duties against separatists' positions[^cite_note-70] was shot down by pro-Russian rebels in the Luhansk region. The Ukrainian government confirmed the downing. The pilot ejected safely and was recovered by friendly forces.[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72]



A MiG-29 armed with an AGM-88 HARM missile

As of 2018, the [Lviv State Aircraft Repair Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lviv_State_Aircraft_Repair_Plant) began domestically upgrading the MiG-29 to have multirole capability, known as the MiG-29MU2. Development was expected to be completed by 2019 and enter production in 2020.[^cite_note-73] The first upgraded MiG-29 was delivered to the Ukrainian Air Force in July 2020.[^cite_note-74] On 29 May 2020, Ukrainian MiG-29s took part in the Bomber Task Force in Europe with American B-1B bombers for the first time in the Black Sea region.[^cite_note-75] In September 2020, B-52 bombers from the [5th Bomb Wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5th_Bomb_Wing) conducted vital integration training with Ukrainian MiG-29s and Su-27s inside Ukraine's airspace.[^cite_note-76][^cite_note-77]



MiG-29s of the [40th Tactical Aviation Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/40th_Tactical_Aviation_Brigade), 2023

During the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine), [Su-27s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Su-27) and MiG-29s were used as air superiority fighters, with ten MiG-29s reported lost on the ground and in the air.[^cite_note-78][^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80][^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82][^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84] In August 2022, a senior U.S. defense official disclosed that the Ukrainians have integrated the [AGM-88 HARM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AGM-88_HARM) missile onto their "MiG aircraft"[^cite_note-85] with video evidence of AGM-88 missiles fired by upgraded Ukrainian MiG-29s released by the Ukrainian Air Force few days later.[^cite_note-86] For a weapon that relies on digital display to fire, the question of how it has been integrated into the MiG-29's analogue displays remains unanswered. The footage shows a commercial GPS having been installed along with a tablet of some kind.[^cite_note-87]

On 13 October 2022, a Ukrainian MiG-29 crashed during a combat mission. Its pilot is claimed to have destroyed a [Shahed-136](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahed-136) drone with his cannon, and it is believed the debris from the drone collided with the aircraft and forced the pilot to eject. Ukrainian sources claim that the pilot shot down five drones and two cruise missiles shortly before the crash. The downed MiG-29 was wearing a livery similar to that of the Ukrainian Falcons display team. According to the Ukrainian State Bureau of Investigation: "the jet collided with debris from a destroyed drone, which caused massive damage to it to the point where it crashed near a village in northeast Vinnytsia. The pilot managed to eject and is currently receiving treatment in the hospital."[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89] On 20 September 2023, a [Ukrainian Air Force](1135769.md) MiG-29 was struck by a [ZALA Lancet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ZALA_Lancet) drone at the Dolgintsevo air base near [Kryvyi Rih](624764.md). A second drone was used as a spotter, recording the first Lancet's impact.[^cite_note-90] Another Ukrainian MiG-29 was lost in [Odesa Oblast](61438266.md) on 10 August 2026, after the aircraft caught fire following the launch of an air-to-air missile while attempting to intercept Russian aerial targets. The pilot ejected successfully and was taken for medical treatment.[^cite_note-91] As of 11 August 2026, 35 Ukrainian MiG-29s have been destroyed (four on the ground) and three have been damaged (two on the ground) by Russian forces.[^cite_note-92]

On 1 October 2026, a Russian missile shot down a Ukrainian MiG-29, killing the pilot, Captain Marat Redvanovych Mambetov, over "eastern Ukraine" during a combat mission. At the time he was the flight commander of the [40th Tactical Aviation Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/40th_Tactical_Aviation_Brigade).[^cite_note-93]

#### Uzbekistan

Uzbekistan inherited 30 single seat (MiG-29As or Cs) and six dual trainers from the Soviet Union following the breakup of the Union. These fighters were originally under the 115th IAP at Kodaidy near Afghanistan [^cite_note-\:9-94] (where they were used to provide aircover for the Soviet Forces in Afghanistan)[^cite_note-\:8-34] but were re-organized in 1993 to form the 61st Fighter Air Brigade, with new three tone camouflage and Air Force titles.[^cite_note-\:9-94] As of 2026, 26 single seat and five dual seat airframes are in service,[^cite_note-i536-3] however the airframes are aging and Uzbekistan is seeking a replacement for their Fulcrums and other Soviet-era jets with the Uzbek Minister of Defense, [Major General Shukhrat Khalmukhamedov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shukhrat_Kholmukhamedov), confirmed the order of new Chinese jets in 2025 with deliveries allegedly having been conducted that year itself.[^cite_note-95][^cite_note-96]

### Export

#### India



[Indian Air Force](403355.md) MiG-29UPGs flying over the [Himalayas](37966.md)

India was the first international customer of the MiG-29, outside of the Warsaw Pact. Despite preference for additional procurement of French made [Mirage 2000s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirage_2000) for the [Indian Air Force](403355.md) (IAF), the Indian government opted for Soviet made MiG-29s. India placed an order for 44 aircraft (40 single-seat MiG-29 9.12Bs and four twin-seat MiG-29UBs) in 1984, and the aircraft was officially inducted into the IAF in 1987.[^cite_note-97] IAF MiG-29s were equipped with the N019 “Slot Back” radar, R-27, R-60 and R-73 missiles.[^cite_note-98] In 1989, an additional order for 20 aircraft was placed, with ten more MiG-29s added in 1994.[^cite_note-SIPRI-99] Since its acquisition, the aircraft has undergone a series of modifications with the addition of new avionics, subsystems, turbofan engines and radars.[^cite_note-100] India signed a US\$888 million deal with Russia in 2005–06 to upgrade all of its MiG-29s, to extend their operational life by 10–15 years.[^cite_note-HT-101] The upgrade program was planned to be completed in four years, and consisted of retrofitting the aircraft with upgraded avionics including replacing the N-019 radar with Phazatron [Zhuk-M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhuk_\(radar\)) radar. Under the deal, the aircraft were also modified to be capable of deploying the [R-77](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-77) missiles, and enabling [mid air refueling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid_air_refueling) to increase endurance.[^cite_note-102] In 2007, [Hindustan Aeronautics Limited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindustan_Aeronautics_Limited) (HAL) was given a license to manufacture 120 [RD-33 series 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RD-33_series_3) turbofan engines for the upgrade.[^cite_note-103]



An upgraded MiG-29UPG of the [Indian Air Force](403355.md)

In March 2008, India signed a renewed agreement with Russia worth \$960 million for upgrading its existing fleet of 69 MiG-29s, which would enable them to operate for another 25 to 40 years.[^cite_note-TOI-104] The upgrades to Indian MiG-29s will be to the MiG-29UPG standard, the first six MiG-29s were to be upgraded in Russia with the remaining 63 MiGs to be upgraded by HAL in India.[^cite_note-TOI-104] The upgrades included a new weapon control system, improved cockpit ergonomics, [DRDO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DRDO)/[DARE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defence_Avionics_Research_Establishment) D-29 electronic warfare system, and upgraded weaponry including air-to-ground missiles and guided bombs.[^cite_note-Upgrade-105][^cite_note-DRDO-106][^cite_note-107] India also awarded a contract to [Israel Aircraft Industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Aircraft_Industries) to provide avionics and subsystems for the upgrade.[^cite_note-TOI-104] India sent the first six of its aircraft to Russia for upgrades in 2008.[^cite_note-Strat-32] The first three aircraft were delivered to India in December 2012, more than two years behind schedule.[^cite_note-108] In March 2009, the IAF expressed concern about the aircraft after 90 Russian MiG-29s were grounded after a crash.[^cite_note-109] In March 2009, the IAF cleared all MiG-29s in its fleet as safe after an extensive inspection.[^cite_note-110] In a disclosure to the [Indian Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Parliament), the [Indian Defence Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Defence_\(India\)) said structural flaws had been identified in the MiG-29s with the possibility of cracks developing in the tail fin due to corrosion. After Russia shared the finding with India, which emerged after the crash of a Russian Air Force MiG-29 in December 2008, a repair and maintenance program was put in place to resolve the issue.[^cite_note-111]



An [Indian Navy](604522.md) MiG-29K landing on *[INS Vikramaditya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INS_Vikramaditya)*

In 2004, India had signed a deal for the acquisition of additional 16 [MiG-29K](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MiG-29K)s for the [Indian Navy](604522.md), to be deployed on [INS *Vikramaditya*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INS_Vikramaditya), the refurbished [aircraft carrier](2219.md) to be purchased from Russia. In January 2010, India and Russia signed a US\$1.2 billion deal for the acquisition of additional 29 MiG-29Ks for the Indian Navy, bringing the total number of MiG-29Ks on order to 45.[^cite_note-Navy-15] The MiG-29K entered service with the Indian Navy on 19 February 2010.[^cite_note-112]

By September 2019, the fleet of 69 IAF MiG-29s were upgraded to UPG standard. The upgrade project involved 6 aircraft being upgraded in Russia and the remaining fleet at the 11th Base Repair and Depot facilities at [Ojhar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ojhar_Air_Force_Station) , which have been increasingly used to maintain the IAF's fleet of MiG-29s and [Su-30MKIs](3793034.md).[^cite_note-113] In 2020, India planned an additional order of 21 MiG-29s, which would be manufactured from airframes built in late 1980s but never assembled, and upgraded before delivery to the Indian Air Force.[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116] On 7 August 2024, IAF issued a [request for proposal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Request_for_proposal) to upgrade 24 MiG-29s to integrate [High Speed Low Drag Bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Speed_Low_Drag_Bomb) with a range of 180 km (110 mi). The upgrade would include installation of hardware such as [bomb racks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bomb_racks) on external [hardpoints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardpoints), and necessary software for the aircraft, with the entire MiG-29 fleet to be modified in phases.[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118]

The Indian Armed Forces have lost about 25 MiG-29 aircraft from 1989 to 2025 due to accidents and other incidents.[^cite_note-119] As of 2026, the Indian Air Force has 65 MiG-29UPGs and 10 MiG-29B trainers, while the Indian Navy has 36 MiG-29Ks and nine MiG-29KUB trainers in service.[^cite_note-120] In early 2026, the IAF proposed to equip its MiG-29s with [ASRAAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ASRAAM) missiles to replace its older R-73 missiles.[^cite_note-121] The ministry of defence invited bids for integrating the missiles onboard 56 MiG-29s, including eight twin-seater variants, with the project proposed to be executed at the [11 Base Repair Depot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nashik_Airport#Ozar_Air_Force_Station) (BRD) at [Nashik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nashik).[^cite_note-122]

On 8 January 2026, Astra Rafael Cosmys, a [joint venture](538782.md) between [Hyderabad](37534.md)-based Astra Microwave Products and [Rafael Advanced Defense Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Advanced_Defense_Systems), announced that they were awarded a contract worth [₹](490783.md)275.27 [crore](121420.md) (US\$29 million) from the Indian Air Force for [avionics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avionics) and [network-centric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Network-centric_warfare) upgrades of its fighter fleet. The contract included the integration of [software-defined radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Software-defined_radio)s (SDR) and installation of network-centric operations (NCO) onto the MiG-29 fleet, including 29 fighters and 7 trainers, as well as the supply of 24 SDR units for [HAL Tejas Mk1A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HAL_Tejas_Mk1A) aircraft. The contract would be executed within 12 months.[^cite_note-123][^cite_note-124] The integration of SDR was initiated for the MiG-29 fleet in July. According to reports, the SDR includes Netcor 1 and 2 systems connected through [MIL-STD-1553](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MIL-STD-1553) bus for NCO capability. The project will be undertaken across 8 batches for over 18 months at 11 BRD, [AFS Adampur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AFS_Adampur) and [AFS Srinagar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srinagar_AFS).[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126]

##### Kargil War

MiG-29s were extensively used by the IAF during the [Kargil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kargil_War) with Pakistan in 1999. They were used to provide escort for the [Mirage 2000s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirage_2000) attacking targets with [laser-guided bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laser-guided_bomb).[^cite_note-127]

##### Balakot strike

Indian MiG-29s were scrambled to intercept PAF aircraft during the Pakistani counter-strike after India struck a target in Pakistan in February 2019.[^cite_note-\:15-128]

##### Operation Sindoor

During the [2025 India–Pakistan conflict](79896622.md), MiG-29s were deployed on forward bases and participated in the operation.[^cite_note-129][^cite_note-\:15-128] The Indian Navy also deployed 15 MiG-29Ks aboard the aircraft carrier *[INS Vikrant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INS_Vikrant_\(2013\))* in the [Arabian Sea](2747.md) in case hostilities expanded.[^cite_note-\:2-16][^cite_note-130]

#### Yugoslavia and Serbia



MiG-29 of the [Serbian Air Force and Air Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Air_Force_and_Air_Defence)

[Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Federal_Republic_of_Yugoslavia) was the first European country outside the Soviet Union to operate the MiG-29. The country received 14 MiG-29s (9.12A) and two MiG-29UBs from the USSR in 1987 and 1988. The MiG-29s were put into service with the 127th Fighter Aviation Squadron, based at [Batajnica Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batajnica_Air_Base), north of [Belgrade](55904.md), Serbia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordonDavison200577-131]

Yugoslav MiG-29s saw little combat during the breakup of Yugoslavia, and were used primarily for ground attacks. Several [Antonov An-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonov_An-2) aircraft used by Croatia were destroyed on the ground at [Čepin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%8Cepin) airfield near [Osijek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osijek), Croatia in 1991 by a Yugoslav MiG-29, with no MiG-29 losses.[^cite_note-ACIG_380-132] At least two MiG-29s carried out an airstrike on [Banski Dvori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banski_Dvori), the official residence of the [Croatian Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_Government), on 7 October 1991.[^cite_note-133]

The MiG-29s continued their service in the subsequent [Federal Republic of Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Republic_of_Yugoslavia). Because of the United Nations [arms embargo](5974443.md) against the country, the condition of the MiG-29s worsened as aircraft were not maintained according to rules and general overhaul scheduled for 1996 and 1997 was not conducted.[^cite_note-134]

Six MiG-29s were shot down during the NATO intervention in the [Kosovo War](16760.md), four by USAF F-15s, one by a USAF F-16, and one by a [Royal Netherlands Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Netherlands_Air_Force) F-16.[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136] However, one aircraft, according to its pilot, was hit by [friendly fire](202484.md) from the ground.[^cite_note-137] Another four were destroyed on the ground.[^cite_note-ejection-history.org.uk-138] One Argentine source claims that a MiG-29 shot down an F-16 on 26 March 1999,[^cite_note-139] but this kill is disputed, as the F-16C in question was said to have crashed in the US that same day.[^cite_note-140]

An MiG-29 9.12A in service with the Yugoslav Air Force during Operation Allied Force in 1999.

A digital representation of a MiG-29 (9.12A) in service with the [Yugoslav Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Force_of_Serbia_and_Montenegro) during [Operation Allied Force](322473.md) in 1999

The [Air Force of Serbia and Montenegro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Force_of_Serbia_and_Montenegro) continued flying its remaining five MiG-29s at a very low rate after the war. In the spring of 2004, news appeared that MiG-29 operations had ceased, because the aircraft could not be maintained,[^cite_note-ACIG_380-132] but later the five remaining airframes were sent to Russia for overhaul. In July 2009, a [Serbian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Air_Force) MiG-29 crashed over Belgrade.[^cite_note-141] The small Serbian MiG-29 fleet along with other jets were grounded for four months during the summer of 2014 due to a battery procurement issue.[^cite_note-142]

In November 2016, Russia had agreed to donate six of its MiG-29s free of charge, if Serbia would pay the repair costs of \$50 million for them.[^cite_note-143] At the end of January 2017, Serbian defense minister [Zoran Đorđević](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoran_%C4%90or%C4%91evi%C4%87_\(politician\)) said that Belarus also agreed to donate eight of its MiG-29s to Serbia on a no-pay basis.[^cite_note-Belarus_donates-144] In early October 2017, Russia completed the delivery of all the six MiG-29s. The aircraft were transferred to Serbia on board an [Antonov An-124](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonov_An-124) transport aircraft.[^cite_note-145] On 25 February 2019, Belarus formally handed four MiG-29s to the Serbian military during a ceremony held at the [558th Aircraft Repair Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/558th_Aircraft_Repair_Plant?action=edit\&redlink=1) in [Baranavichy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baranavichy). This increased the Serbian Air Force's fleet to 14 MiG-29s.[^cite_note-Serbia_received_4_MiG-29s-146] Serbia plans to spend about €180–230 million on modernization of its entire MiG-29 fleet.[^cite_note-Serbia_MiG-29_modernization-147][^cite_note-148] As of 2021, the Serbian Air Force operates 14 MiG-29s.[^cite_note-149] Images taken in March 2026 showed Serbian MiG-29s using [LS6 Precision Guided Bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LS_PGB) (a conversion kit for unguided bombs) and the [CM-400 Air to Surface Missile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CM-400) which were previously integrated.[^cite_note-SMAWC-150][^cite_note-SMAAWC-151][^cite_note-152]

#### Germany

[East Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Germany) bought 24 MiG-29s (20 MiG-29As, four MiG-29UBs), which entered service in 1988–1989 in 1./JG3 "*[Wladimir Komarow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Komarov)*" in [Preschen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preschen_Airfield) in [Brandenburg](3765.md).[^cite_note-wapj4_p86-153] After the fall of the [Berlin Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Wall) in November 1989 and [reunification of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reunification_of_Germany) in October 1990, the MiG-29s and other aircraft of the East German *[Air Forces of the National People's Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Forces_of_the_National_People's_Army)* were integrated into the [West German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Germany) [Luftwaffe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Air_Force).[^cite_note-154] Initially the 1./JG3 kept its designation. In April 1991 both 1./JG3's MiG-29 squadrons were reorganised into the MiG-29 test wing ("*Erprobungsgeschwader MiG-29*"), which became [JG73 "*Steinhoff*"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taktisches_Luftwaffengeschwader_73) and was transferred to [Laage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laage) near [Rostock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rostock) in June 1993.



[East German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Forces_of_the_National_People's_Army) MiG-29 operating at [Preschen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preschen_Airfield) in 1990

The [Federation of American Scientists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_American_Scientists) claims the MiG-29 is equal to, or better than the F-15C in short aerial engagements because of the [Helmet Mounted Weapons Sight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helmet_mounted_display) (HMS) and better maneuverability at slow speeds.[^cite_note-FAS_MiG-29-155]This was demonstrated when MiG-29s of the German Air Force participated in joint [DACT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DACT) exercises with US fighters.[^cite_note-Lucia-156][^cite_note-Code_One-157] The HMS was a great help, allowing the Germans to achieve a lock on any target the pilot could see within the missile field of view, including those almost 45 degrees off [boresight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_harmonisation).[^cite_note-158] However, the German pilots who flew the MiG-29 admitted that while the Fulcrum was more maneuverable at slow speeds than the [F-15 Eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-15_Eagle), [F-16 Fighting Falcon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-16_Fighting_Falcon), [F-14 Tomcat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-14_Tomcat), and [F/A-18 Hornet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F/A-18_Hornet) and its [Vympel R-73](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vympel_R-73) dogfight missile system was superior to the [AIM-9 Sidewinder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-9_Sidewinder) of the time, in engagements that went into the beyond visual range arena, the German pilots found it difficult to multi-task locking and firing the MiG-29's [Vympel R-27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vympel_R-27) missile (German MiG-29s did not have access to the more advanced [Vympel R-77](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vympel_R-77) that equips more advanced MiG-29 versions) while trying to avoid the longer range and advanced search and track capabilities of the American fighters' radars and [AIM-120 AMRAAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-120_AMRAAM). The Germans also stated that the American fighters had the advantage in both night and bad weather combat conditions. The Luftwaffe's assessment of the MiG-29 was that the Fulcrum was best used as a point defense interceptor over cities and military installations, not for fighter sweeps over hostile airspace. This assessment ultimately led Germany to not deploy its MiG-29s in the [Kosovo War](16760.md) during [Operation Allied Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Allied_Force), though Luftwaffe pilots who flew the MiG-29 admitted that even if they were permitted to fly combat missions over the former Yugoslavia they would have been hampered by the lack of NATO-specific communication tools and [identification friend or foe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identification_friend_or_foe) systems.[^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160]



[German Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Air_Force) MiG-29

Beginning in 1993, the German MiGs were stationed with [JG73 "*Steinhoff*"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taktisches_Luftwaffengeschwader_73) in [Laage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laage) near [Rostock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rostock). During the service in the German Air Force, one MiG-29 ("29+09") was destroyed in an accident on 25 June 1996 due to pilot error. By 2003, German Air Force pilots had flown over 30,000 hours in the MiG-29. In September 2003, 22 of the 23 remaining machines were sold to the [Polish Air Force](602835.md) for the symbolic price of €1 per item.[^cite_note-PL-161] The last aircraft were transferred in August 2004.[^cite_note-162] The 23rd MiG-29 ("29+03") was put on display at Laage.[^cite_note-Bundeswehr-163]

#### Libya

In the second half of the 1980s, the Soviet Union offered the MiG-29 to Libya. The offer was turned down, as the weapons system and radar of the MiG-29 were assessed as similar to those of the [MiG-23MLD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan-Gurevich_MiG-23) already in service with the [Libyan Arab Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Arab_Air_Force). The MiG-29's price was also deemed much too high.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECooper2018b47-164]

##### Libyan Civil War

In 2020, it was reported that MiG-29 aircraft were flown by forces aligned with [Khalifa Haftar](31227894.md) in Libya.[^cite_note-165] On 11 September 2020, [United States Africa Command](9347800.md) stated that two MiG-29s, operated by Russian speaking personnel, crashed in Libya for unknown reasons, the first on 28 June 2020, the second on 7 September 2020.[^cite_note-TRMHC-166] These were operated by [Wagner Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wagner_Group) and crashed near [Sirte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirte), [Libya](17633.md).[^cite_note-TRMHC-166] It was announced that MiG-29s and Su-24s are to be delivered to the [Libyan Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Air_Force) from Russia.[^cite_note-167]

#### Peru



MiG-29SE of the [Peruvian Air Force](1040948.md)

The [Peruvian Air Force](1040948.md) acquired 21 MiG-29S fighters from [Belarus](3457.md) in 1996,[^cite_note-168] as part of a package that also included 18 [Su-25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Su-25) attack aircraft. The following year, an additional 3 MiG-29 aircraft were acquired from Russia.[^cite_note-169] At the same time, Peru contracted with Mikoyan to upgrade 8 aircraft to the MiG-29SMP standard, with an option to upgrade the remainder of the Peruvian inventory. Peru contracted RAC MiG to overhaul and upgrade 19 of its MiG-29s in August 2008 for a contract valued at 106 million dollars, but the process was delayed when technicians found the jets were in worse condition than expected upon arrival in Peru. The first eight of these jets were delivered in 2012 and are expected to last for 20 years.[^cite_note-170] The Peruvian MiG-29s are based at [FAP Captain José Abelardo Quiñones González International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FAP_Captain_Jos%C3%A9_Abelardo_Qui%C3%B1ones_Gonz%C3%A1lez_International_Airport) in northern Peru, equipping *Escuadrón Aéreo* 612 (Fighter Squadron 612 "Fighting Roosters").[^cite_note-171]

#### Poland



A [Polish Air Force](602835.md) MiG-29 with a USAF [F-16](11642.md)

The first 12 MiG-29s delivered to Poland were nine MiG-29As and three MiG-29UBs in 1989–1990. The aircraft were based at [Mińsk Mazowiecki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mi%C5%84sk_Mazowiecki) and used by the 1st Fighter Aviation Regiment, which was reorganized in 2001 as *[1 Eskadra Lotnictwa Taktycznego](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1_Eskadra_Lotnictwa_Taktycznego)* (*1. elt*), or 1st Tactical Squadron (TS). In 1995–1996, 10 used examples were acquired from the Czech Republic (nine MiG-29As, one MiG-29UB). After the retirement of its MiG-23s in 1999, and MiG-21s in 2004, Poland was left for a time with only these 22 MiG-29s in the interceptor role.

Of the 22 MiG-29s Poland received from the [German Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Air_Force) in 2004, a total of 14 were overhauled and taken into service. They were used to equip the 41st Tactical Squadron (*41. elt*), replacing its MiG-21s. As of 2008, Poland was the biggest NATO MiG-29 user. Poland had 31 active MiG-29s (25 MiG-29As, six MiG-29UBs) as of 2017.[^cite_note-172] They are stationed with the [1st Tactical Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_Tactical_Squadron) at the [23rd Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/23rd_Air_Base) near [Mińsk Mazowiecki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mi%C5%84sk_Mazowiecki) and the [41st TS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/41st_Tactical_Squadron) at the [22nd Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/22nd_Air_Base) near [Malbork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malbork).

There have been unconfirmed reports that Poland has at one point leased a MiG-29 from its own inventory to Israel for evaluation and the aircraft has since been returned to Poland, as suggested by photographs of a MiG-29 in Israeli use. Three Polish MiG-29As were reported in Israel for evaluation between April and May 1997 in the [Negev Desert](142273.md). On 7 September 2011, the [Polish Air Force](602835.md) awarded a contract to the WZL 2 company to modernise its MiG-29 fleet to be compatible with Polish F-16s.[^cite_note-173]

Four MiG-29s from 1. elt participated in the [Baltic Air Policing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Air_Policing) mission in 2006, while 41. elt aircraft did so in 2008, 2010 and 2012. Polish MiG-29s played the aggressor role in the NATO Tactical Leadership Programme (TLP) joint training program in [Albacete](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albacete) in 2011, 2012 and 2013.[^cite_note-174]

On 18 December 2017, a MiG-29 crash-landed in a forest near the [23rd Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/23rd_Air_Base) while performing a landing approach.[^cite_note-175] The pilot did not eject, but survived the crash with minor injuries. This was the first crash of a MiG-29 during its nearly three decades long operational history in the Polish Air Force.[^cite_note-176] On 6 July 2018, another MiG-29 crashed near [Pasłęk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pas%C5%82%C4%99k), with its pilot dying in an ejection attempt. Technical issues are suspected to have played a role in the crash.[^cite_note-177] Another crash followed on 4 March 2019. This time the pilot ejected and survived.[^cite_note-178]

On 8 March 2022, Poland announced a willingness to transfer their operational fleet of MiG-29 aircraft to the US via the [Ramstein Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramstein_Air_Base), in exchange for aircraft of a similar role and operational capability, with the intent of transferring the MiG-29s to Ukraine to use in the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md).[^cite_note-179]

On 16 March 2023, Polish President [Andrzej Duda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrzej_Duda) announced that Poland would transfer four operational MiG-29s to Ukraine, with the understanding that additional aircraft would be delivered after servicing and preparation. Poland is the first NATO country to provide Ukraine with fighter aircraft.[^cite_note-180]

On 13 April, German Defence Minister [Boris Pistorius](69097811.md) announced that Germany had approved the transfer of five MiG-29s to Ukraine. German approval was necessary because these aircraft belonged to the [German Democratic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Democratic_Republic), which were then transferred to Poland in 2004.[^cite_note-181]

#### Iraq



[Iraqi Air Force](1817852.md) MiG-29 fighter

Iraq received a number of MiG-29 fighters and used them to engage Iranian equivalent opponents during the later stages of the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War).

##### Invasion of Kuwait and Gulf War

By August 1990, at the time of the [Invasion of Kuwait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_invasion_of_Kuwait), the [Iraqi Air Force](1817852.md) had received 37 MiG-29 (9.12B) Fulcrum-As. Iraq was reportedly unsatisfied that they did not receive the [R-73](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-73_\(missile\)) and [R-27T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-27_\(air-to-air_missile\)) missiles that Coalition intelligence had assessed as a great threat, instead receiving [R-60MK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-60_\(missile\)) missiles. As a result, they did not order any more aircraft. Iraq was reportedly able to modify their MiG-29s to carry both drop tanks and the TMV-002 Remora ECM pod.[^cite_note-182]

MiG-29s saw combat in the 1991 [Persian Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) with the [Iraqi Air Force](1817852.md). Five MiG-29s were shot down by USAF F-15s.[^cite_note-steve_davies-183] As per some sources, the Iraqi Air Force claimed a [British](25679.md) [Panavia Tornado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panavia_Tornado), *ZA467*, was shot down in northwestern Iraq by a MiG-29.[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-Iraqi_air_victories_during_Gulf_War-185] British sources claim this Tornado to have crashed on 22 January on a mission to [Ar Rutbah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ar_Rutbah).[^cite_note-asn-za467-186] Another Tornado is also claimed by an Iraqi MiG-29, this time, and IDS variant belonging to the [AMI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Air_Force) from either the 4th or 5th Fighter Squadron, again using an R-60 missile.[^cite_note-187]

Other Iraqi air-to-air kills are reported in Russian sources, where the US claims other cases of combat damage, such as a B-52 which the US claims was hit by friendly fire, when an [AGM-88](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AGM-88_HARM) high-speed, anti-radiation missile (HARM) homed on the fire-control radar of the B-52's tail gun; the bomber returned to base and was subsequently renamed "*In HARM's Way*".[^cite_note-188] It is believed that an [F-111 Aardvark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Dynamics_F-111_Aardvark) was hit by a missile fired by a MiG-29 but it was able to return to base.[^cite_note-189] A MiG-29 from the 4th or 5th TFS also had a less fortunate kill against a friendly aircraft, a MiG-23ML using a R-27R missile on the 17th of January, 1991. This case of fratricide was not the first of this type, but a MiG-29 of the 5th TFS shot down another MiG-29 of the same unit using an R-60MK in April 1988, though the circumstances are not entirely known.[^cite_note-190]

##### Post Gulf War

Iraq's original fleet of 37 MiG-29s was reduced to 12 after the Gulf War. One MiG-29 was damaged, and between four and nine were reportedly flown to Iran.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon2006483-191] The remaining 12 aircraft were withdrawn from use in 1995 because the engines needed to be overhauled but Iraq could not send them off for that work.[^cite_note-192] After the American-led [2003 invasion of Iraq](201936.md) and disbandment of the [Ba'athist Iraqi](26215470.md) [Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Army) in May of the same year,[^cite_note-193] the remaining Soviet-made and Chinese-made fighters of the Iraqi Air Force had been decommissioned.

#### Iran

After the Iran-Iraq war, Iran placed an order for MiG-29s from the Soviet Union. The first batch of the aircraft was delivered in 1990 and became operational with the [Iran Air Force](412792.md) on 7 October 1990. While the exact number of purchases is unknown, at least 24 MiG-29s (20As and 4UBs) were sold to Iran.[^cite_note-\:17-194] During the Persian Gulf War, at least four and up to nine Iraqi MiG-29s were flown over to Iran for safekeeping from [allied forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition), which Iran confiscated later as reparations for the damage caused during the earlier Iran-Iraq war. Some sources say seven (with three crashes along the way) were sent over.[^cite_note-\:17-194] Later, when Moldova attempted to sell its MiG-29s in 1992, Iran was touted as one of the possible buyers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon1999142-195] Iran set up an overhaul and refurbishment program for their MiG-29s at Mehrabad by Iranian Aircraft Industry with Indian assistance after they experienced difficulties with their fleet. A number of MiG-29s received bolt on refueling probes, created new drop tanks and wired the MiGs for western origin air to air missiles with modified MiG-29s receiving a new camouflage (light grey and light blue).[^cite_note-196]

Iran has used MiG-29s continuously since their induction, but has lost some in incidents. During their delivery flight, one MiG-29A crashed when its Soviet pilot got lost flying the aircraft to Iran and was forced to eject. Another MiG-29A was lost shortly after when a MiG-29A flown by an Iranian pilot crashed while flying low with the Iranians claiming mechanical failure but a MiG inquiry concluded that the pilot experienced disorientation and flew into a mountain. A single attiration replacement was delivered at some point in the 1990s.[^cite_note-\:17-194] More recent incidents include one in 2016 and another in December 2019 due to a crash in the [Sabalan Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabalan).[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198]

As of March 2025, Iran was estimated to have an inventory of 18 MiG-29s.[^cite_note-199] In September 2025, as per a statement by a member of the Iranian [Supreme National Security Council](6295948.md) to the [Islamic Consultative Assembly](383276.md), Iran reportedly received unknown number of MiG-29s from Russia.[^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201] The Iranian MiG-29s are equipped with Soviet N019 Rubin radar, and are capable of carrying [R-27R](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-27_\(air-to-air_missile\)) and [R-73RMD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-73_\(missile\)) air to air missiles.

##### 2026 Iran War

During the [2026 Iran war](82533582.md), the MiG-29s were used to intercept Israeli and US missiles, [attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack_drone) and [surveillance drones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surveillance_drone) over Iranian cities and military sites.[^cite_note-202]

#### Syria

Syria was the second export customer of the MiG-29, receiving them before most of the [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact) nations did, with the Syrian Defence Minister Mustafa Tlass initially seeking to acquire up to 150 MiG-29s in order to modernize the [SyAAF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Air_Force). The very first Fulcrums arrived in Syria in June or July 1987 as a batch of 16 and more were delivered in the late 1980s.[^cite_note-\:1-203] A total of up to 48 MiG-29As and UBs were delivered in the original batches, with 16 more delivered in the mid to late 1990s (these reportedly airframes built for Iraq in 1990-91 but undelivered due to sanctions and the Gulf War). Over time, these airframes were upgraded to an unknown standard.[^cite_note-Israeli-Syrian_Shadow-Boxing.-204]

##### Syrian - Israeli skirmishes

Syrian Arab Air Force MiG-29s have sometimes encountered [Israeli](177619.md) fighter and reconnaissance aircraft. Western sources state that two Israeli F-15Cs shot down two MiG-29As on 2 June 1989 under unclear circumstances thought this is not confirmed by Syrian sources.[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-Israeli-Syrian_Shadow-Boxing.-204] Some reports claim that between 1990 and 1991, several air battles occurred between the Syrians and the Israelis and one MiG-29 is alleged to have been lost in those engagements, with Israeli losses in these clashes claimed but unverified.[^cite_note-\:1-203]

Further reports claim that on 14 September 2001 two Syrian Air Force MiG-29s were shot down by two Israeli F-15Cs while the MiGs were intercepting an Israeli reconnaissance aircraft off the coast of Lebanon. However, both Syria and Israel deny that this occurred, yet unofficial sources continue to hold that an engagement did take place, with the names of the Syrian pilots given being Maj. Arshad Midhat Mubarak, and Capt. Ahmad al-Khatab and both were reported rescued from the Mediterranean. It is stated that the battle occurred when an Israeli Air Force [Boeing 707 SIGINT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_707) aircraft, escorted by two F-15Cs armed with advanced [Python Mk.IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_\(missile\)) missiles, was conducting a routine surveillance mission along the Lebanese and Syrian coasts, monitoring telecommunications and radar activity near Tripoli and Hamidiyah. Such missions, conducted twice weekly, were typically shadowed by Syrian interceptors—usually MiG-23s or MiG-29s—that maintained a safe distance and showed no hostile intent. However, at 09\:14 that day, two Syrian MiG-29s suddenly turned aggressively toward the Israeli formation, prompting the F-15s to order the 707 to retreat and activate ECM, while reinforcements were scrambled. After warnings on the international distress frequency were ignored, one F-15 shot down the lead MiG-29 with a Python Mk.IV missile, while the second MiG, attempting to retreat, was destroyed by a close-range [AIM-9M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-9_Sidewinder) Sidewinder, both crashing into the Mediterranean and the pilots were retrieved.[^cite_note-Israeli-Syrian_Shadow-Boxing.-204][^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207]

##### Syrian Civil War

Syrian MiG-29s entered the [Syrian Civil War](30741795.md) in late October 2013, attacking [Free Syrian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Syrian_Army) insurgents with unguided rockets and bombs in [Damascus](8914.md)[^cite_note-208] and during the war, MiG-29SM fighters (a deep upgrade by Russia of the platform) were delivered to the SyAAF. A Syrian MiG-29SM crashed on 5 March 2020 near [Shayrat Airbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shayrat_Airbase). Marking the first crash of the plane in the Syrian Air Force since 2001. According to *avia.pro* the aircraft may have been shot down by [MANPADS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MANPADS) operated by the [Syrian Opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Opposition).[^cite_note-209][^cite_note-210] After the fall of the Assad Ba'athist Regime, the remaining assets of the SyAAF were largely taken over the opposition forces, which formed the new government. The Israelis - who were concerned about the instability with the new regime - destroyed at least 30 MiG-29s as part of [Operation Bashan Arrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Bashan_Arrow) which involved Israeli Strikes on Syrian Defence Infrastructure including air bases,[^cite_note-211] and the condition of any surviving aircraft is uncertain.

#### Malaysia



A Malaysian MiG-29 in 2012

Russia and [Malaysia](3607937.md) signed an agreement for the purchase of 16 single and two dual MiG-29s on 7 June 1994 for a total value of \$560 Million with the aircraft delivered to include a number of modifications including improved engines, a stronger airframe, higher gross weight, increased ordnance load, and more capable avionics (including an upgraded radar). The single seaters were set to be retrofitted with a refueling probe post-delivery. These fighters were not new built fighters however, but were rather white tails (aircraft that were already built but without a paying customer) as neither the Soviet or Russian governments had paid for these fighters originally manufactured in 1989–1990. They were stored at [Tretyakovo aerodrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tretyakovo_aerodrome), overhauled and modified they were delivered on schedule. They are serialed M43-01 and 02 (for the dual seat trainers) and -03 to -18 for the single seaters, and are referred to as MiG-29Ns (*Fulcrum-As*).[^cite_note-\:14-212]

The fighters were used to equip the No.17 Squadron "Typhoons" and No. 19 Squadron "Cobras", with the first two delivered airframes (single seaters M43-03 and M-43-04) delivered by an AN-124 Langkawi Airbase near Kuala Lumpur.[^cite_note-\:14-212] The [Royal Malaysian Air Force](1137609.md) operated 18 MiG-29N/NUB aircraft from 1995 to 2017 out of its [air base in Kuantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RMAF_Kuantan_Air_Base), all belonging to the 1st Air Division HQ based out of Kuala Lumpur[^cite_note-213]
