Malaysian MiG-29s would participate in Exercise *Jagukh* in the Spring of 1996, and with the [SEATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia_Treaty_Organization) members in their largest exercise up to that time, *Exercise Flying Fish '97* on 12–30 April 1997 involving over 12,000 personnel in the South China Sea, where the MiGs were especially praised, and they participated alongside Malaysia's [F-5Es](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-5E). They also received technical assistance from MAPO MiG personnel on the ground at this time with 14 of the 16 single seat aircraft ready at this time. One MIG-29, M43-05 was displayed at an airshow in [Jakarta](16275.md), [Indonesia](14579.md) between 22 and 30 June 1996.[^cite_note-\:14-212]

It was planned that the aircraft would be upgraded with glass cockpits around 2003, when they also adopted camouflage patterns that the [Su-30MKMs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi_Su-30MKM) (which were being newly inducted) were given.[^cite_note-\:14-212] In 2009, it was announced that the jets would be gradually phased out by the end of 2010 as a result of increasing maintenance costs, which would save approximately \$76 million annually.[^cite_note-214] The Malaysian defence ministry subsequently went back on its position and announced it would keep ten planes in service and only retire the remaining six.[^cite_note-215] In 2013, it was announced that the MiG-29 fleet would be retired by 2015.[^cite_note-216] However, delays with its Multi-Role Combat Aircraft (MRCA) program meant the jets were kept in service and set to be upgraded to extend their lifespan, but these plans ultimately did not materialise and they were finally retired in 2017.[^cite_note-MGM29-217][^cite_note-218]

One crashed in July 1998 (M43-17)[^cite_note-\:14-212] as a result of hydraulic and battery failures, and another in 2004 after its engine caught on fire after takeoff. Both pilots ejected and survived.[^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220]

#### North Korea



A North Korean MiG-29 intercepting a USAF RC-135S in 2003

The [Korean People's Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_People's_Air_Force) is believed to operate about 18 MiG-29s which are assigned to the 55th Air Regiment based at [Sunchon Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunchon_Airport). In addition to 13 MiG-29 (9.12B) Fulcrum As and 2 Fulcrum B trainers that were delivered in 1987, North Korea also became the only Cold War export customer and licensed manufacturer of the Fulcrum-C.[^cite_note-Mitzer-2020-221] Called the MiG-29S-13 (9.13B) Fulcrum-C, they were delivered to North Korea from the USSR/Russia between 1991 and 1992 in knock down parts. Only three S-13s were completed due to Russia refusing to supply more parts to North Korea. The first locally built Fulcrum-C flew on 15 April 1993.[^cite_note-Mitzer-2020-221] These were first encountered and photographed by the US Air Force in March 2003 when a pair of KPAF MiG-29s intercepted a [RC-135S Cobra Ball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_RC-135) [reconnaissance aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconnaissance_aircraft).[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223]

On 17 May 2025, the North Korean MiG-29 was seen testing a new active radar homing missile which resembled the American [AIM-120](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-120_AMRAAM) and Chinese [PL-12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PL-12). The missile seemed to outperform the given Soviet-era MiG-29's radar in terms of range.[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225] In November 2025, during an event marking the 80th anniversary of the air force, the North Korean MiG-29s were spotted sporting a new short range infrared air-to-air missile. The missiles were noted to being similar in configuration to the German [IRIS-T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IRIS-T), Japanese [Type-04](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AAM-5_\(Japanese_missile\)), and the Chinese [PL-10E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PL-10).[^cite_note-226]

#### Sudan



[Sudanese Air Force](550818.md) MiG-29SE

Sudan took delivery of 12 MiG-29s from Russia (though some earlier sources indicated that these were Ukrainian machines) in July 2004, which were used to form the No.2 Squadron and comprised 10 MiG-29SEs (Project 9.13Bs) and two MiG-29UB trainers. Images of one airframe before delivery exist, serialed 623 (black).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon2006499–500-227] An anonymous Russian source claimed that the aircraft had been delivered before 2004.[^cite_note-sudan-228] On 14 November 2008 Sudanese Ministry of Defence admitted that Sudan had received 12 MiG-29s from Russia.[^cite_note-sudan-228]

##### Darfur War

There have been occasional claims regarding the use of [Sudanese Air Force](550818.md) MiG-29s against insurgent forces in [Darfur](605837.md). However, whereas [Mi-24](384591.md) combat helicopters as well as [Nanchang A-5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanchang_Q-5) or, more recently, [Su-25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Su-25) ground-attack aircraft have been spotted and photographed on Darfurian airfields, no MiG-29s have been observed. On 10 May 2008, a Darfur rebel group, the [Justice and Equality Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_and_Equality_Movement) (JEM) mounted an [assault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_attack_on_Omdurman_and_Khartoum) on the Sudanese capital. During this action, the JEM shot down a Sudanese Air Force MiG-29 with 12.7 and 14.5 mm (0.500 and 0.571 in) heavy machine gun fire while it was attacking a convoy of vehicles in the [Khartoum](17320.md) suburb of [Omdurman](100414.md). The aircraft was piloted by a Russian mercenary. He was killed in action as his parachute did not open after ejecting.[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231]

##### 2012 South Sudan conflict

During the brief [2012 South Sudan–Sudan border conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heglig_Crisis), on 4 April 2012, Sudan People's Liberation Army (SPLA) claimed the downing of a Sudanese MiG-29 using antiaircraft guns. The Sudan government denied the claim.[^cite_note-232] On 16 April 2012, the SPLA issued a second claim about the downing of a Sudanese MiG-29.[^cite_note-233] It is not clear if this second claim referred to the previous one.

##### Sudanese Civil War

On 15 April 2023, a Sudanese MiG-29 was captured on film firing missiles over Khartoum during a skirmish with paramilitary forces.[^cite_note-234] On 25 May 2023, a Sudanese MiG-29 was filmed being shot down by the [Rapid Support Forces](43053874.md) (RSF) over [Omdurman](100414.md). The pilot ejected and survived, although he was wounded and captured.[^cite_note-235] A MiG-29 of the Sudanese Air Force shot down a transport aircraft contracted by the United Arab Emirates (who were supporting the RSF) that was carrying north of 40 [Colombian](5222.md) mercenaries near [Nyala Airport](9265150.md) on 7 August 2025, this being the second such transport lost (Friendly fire by the RSF is believed to have been the cause of another loss the year before).[^cite_note-236]

#### Eritrea

The Eritrean Airforce acquired MiG-29s shortly after their independence from Ethiopia as part of an arms modernization programs from Russia (one source states Ukraine), originally acquiring ten to twelve[^cite_note-\:5-237] and acquired six more from Moldova in 2003. Different sources disagree on whether Eritrea bought MiG-29s to counter Ethiopian Su-27s or if Ethiopia bought Su-27s to counter their MiG-29s, but both were at around the same time.[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239][^cite_note-auto-240]

##### Ethiopian - Eritrean (Badme) War

Eritrean Fulcrums were deployed as part of the 5th Squadron based out of Asmara during the Ethiopian-Eritrean War, and were sometimes flown by mercenary pilots from the former USSR. During the 1998-2000 [Eritrean-Ethiopian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eritrean-Ethiopian_War), a number of Eritrean MiG-29s engaged Ethiopian [Su-27s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Su-27) piloted by Russian mercenaries and local pilots trained by Russian instructors and Eritrean MiG-29s were used to intercept Ethiopian air support and strike packages.[^cite_note-241][^cite_note-242]

During the [February 1999 Eritrean - Ethiopian Aerial Clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_1999_Eritrean%E2%80%93Ethiopian_aerial_clashes), Eritrean MiG-29s engaged Ethiopian Su-27s for the first time on February 21, resulting in damage to one MiG-29 and further engagements occurred later in the same month on 25 and 26 February. Each of these engagements resulted in the loss of one Eritrean MiG-29 in each encounter, both pilots were lost. Ethiopia claims between five and seven MiG-29s throughout 1999–2000 by their Su-27s, but only the two lost in February 1999 can be confirmed. Up to 29 R-27 missiles were fired in these engagements, and represented the first such long range combat between fourth Generation fighters.[^cite_note-\:5-237][^cite_note-\:6-243][^cite_note-Eritrean-244]

There are also some other reports of Eritrean MiG-29s shooting down Ethiopian aircraft, the claimed kills for Eritrean MiG-29s are four MiG-21MFs, and one to three [MiG-23s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan-Gurevich_MiG-23) (including one MiG-23BN on the 25th of February 1999 with two R-27Rs, possibly the first kill with that missile type) and at least one [Su-25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi_Su-25).[^cite_note-\:6-243][^cite_note-Eritrean-244][^cite_note-auto-240] The claim that an Eritrean MiG-29 shot down an Ethiopian Su-25 is disputed, since the Ethiopian Su-25TK in question was damaged in an accident in May 2000, is actually stored and used for spares at [Bishoftu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishoftu) Air Base.[^cite_note-245]

##### Later service and defections

It was reported that in 2016, an Eritrean MiG-29UB defected to Ethiopia with two pilots, though details surrounding the event are unclear.[^cite_note-246] None are reported to be active as of 2024, with sanctions in place and Eritrea has since acquired Su-27s.[^cite_note-World_Air_Forces_2023-56]

#### Cuba

The [Cuban Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolutionary_Air_and_Air_Defense_Force) initially sought to acquire between 35 and 40 MiG-29s from the Soviet Union, enough to equip two squadrons or one aviation regiment. This was revised down to fourteen aircraft due to economic conditions - 12 MiG-29As and two MiG-29UBs. The Soviet Union was initially hesitant to provide Cuba with fulcrums due to warming relations with the United States but eventually agreed to. These supplemented other fighter types in Cuban service like the [MiG-21s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan-Gurevich_MiG-21) and [MiG-23s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan-Gurevich_MiG-23), but deliveries were incomplete by the fall of the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-247] As of 2026, none are believed to be flying.[^cite_note-i536-3]

##### Brothers to the Rescue shootdown

A [Cuban MiG-29UB shot down](44792004.md) two [Cessna 337s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cessna_337) belonging to the organisation [Brothers to the Rescue](1613558.md) in 1996 after it was vectored to intercept them following a MiG-23 outside of Cuban territorial waters after the aircraft approached Cuban airspace with a third Cessna escaping.[^cite_note-UMN_86-99-248]

#### United States

In 1997, the US purchased 21 Moldovan MiG-29 aircraft under the [Nunn–Lugar Cooperative Threat Reduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nunn%E2%80%93Lugar_Cooperative_Threat_Reduction) program. Fourteen were MiG-29S models, which are equipped with an active radar jammer in its spine and are capable of being armed with nuclear weapons. Part of the US' motive to purchase these aircraft was to prevent them from being sold to Iran.[^cite_note-link-249] This purchase could also provide the tactical fighter communities of the [USAF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USAF), the [USN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USN) and the [USMC](17349325.md) with a working evaluation and data for the MiG-29, and possibly for use in dissimilar air combat training. Such information may prove valuable in any future conflicts and can aid in the design and testing of current and future weapons platforms. In late 1997, the MiGs were delivered to the National Air and Space Intelligence Center (NASIC) at [Wright-Patterson Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wright-Patterson_Air_Force_Base) in Ohio, though many of the former Moldovan MiG-29s are believed to have been [scrapped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft_boneyard). Some of these MiG-29s are currently on open display at [Nellis AFB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nellis_AFB), Nevada; [NAS Fallon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAS_Fallon), Nevada; [Goodfellow AFB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goodfellow_AFB), Texas; and [Wright-Patterson AFB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wright-Patterson_AFB), Ohio.

The private defense contractor AirUSA (later known as [Ravn Aerospace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ravn_Aerospace)), based at [Quincy, Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quincy,_Illinois), imported two MiG-29UB trainers from Kyrgyzstan into the US in 1996.[^cite_note-250] Their first MiG-29 was restored and made airworthy in 2010, having its first flight on 10 December.[^cite_note-251] As of 2020, the company also has acquired two single-seat MiG-29 as spare parts donors.[^cite_note-252]

#### Yemen

After the end of the [1994 civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemeni_Civil_War_\(1994\)), newly reunified [Yemen](350939.md) inherited several intact MiG-29s, bought by South Yemen a few months earlier.[^cite_note-253] In 1995–1996, Yemen also received two additional jets from Kazakhstan.[^cite_note-HSOY_2_14-55] In 2001, a major arms deal including the purchase of up to 36 upgraded MiG-29s was signed, with deliveries starting in June 2002.[^cite_note-HSOY_2_14-55] Equipped with N019MP radar and an advanced fire control system, they became the most advanced combat aircraft in the Yemeni Air force arsenal. They are compatible with [Kh-31](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kh-31)P and [Kh-29](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kh-29)T guided air-to-ground missiles, as well as [R-77](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-77) air-to-air missiles.[^cite_note-HSOY_2_14-55]

#### Bangladesh

Bangladesh first ordered its MiG-29s in 1999 under a deal worth between \$115 and \$125 Million with Russia, including airframes and ground support, the support of 12 Russian personnel in Bangladesh, and initial training for 10 pilots and 70 technicians, which proved politically contentious. The aircraft were split as six MiG-29SEs (Project 9.12SE) and two UB trainers (Project 9.51). The first four were delivered in December 1999 (two on the 25th and two on the 28th) with the remaining four in February 2000, assigned to the [5th Squadron "Supersonics"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No._5_Squadron_BAF?action=edit\&redlink=1) at [Bashar Airbase (Tejgoan Airfield)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tejgaon_Airport) near Dhaka, officially entering service in March 2000.[^cite_note-\:16-254] They are now operated by the [8th Squadron "Vigilants"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No._8_Squadron_BAF?action=edit\&redlink=1) at BAF base [Bir Uttom Khandaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BAF_Base_Bir_Uttom_A._K._Khandker), near Dhaka.[^cite_note-255] Of the six MiG-29SEs delivered to the Bangladeshis (serialed 36100, 36501–507), one (36501) was an ex-MAPO demonstrator (the 999 "Silver" demonstrator). The two UBs were given the serials 28264 and 28375.[^cite_note-\:16-254]

Air Vice Marshal Jamal Uddin Ahmed stated in an interview on 5 July 1999 that these aircraft would be useful if Bangladesh ever had an external threat, though he admitted it was not the case at the moment. The purchase of the aircraft was controversial within Bangladesh, with the [Leader of the Official Opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition), [Begum Khaleda Jia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaleda_Zia), alleging that the aircraft may have been bought due to corruption or Indian interference, with the possibility of reliance on India and Russia for spares. The government responded stating that there were no irregularities, that Bangladesh operated MiG aircraft from its earliest days as a nation (acquiring MiG-21s in 1972) and pointing out that Bangladesh had for a long time acquired aircraft from other nations, such at [J-7s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chengdu_J-7) and that nobody had objected when they acquired F-6s from Pakistan and China. The then Prime Minister, [Sheikh Hasina](623439.md), also stated the aircraft were acquired at a steep discount, and were the cheapest option (that nobody would have sold them aircraft at a lower rate). Bangladesh had earlier attempted to acquire F-16s from the Americans, as per her testimony, and had been turned down (with the Americans stating they had no use in the subcontinent). She also stated that they would gladly take the 16 F-16s they had originally asked for. In the mid-2000s, there was some talk of selling the MiGs due to their upkeep costs.[^cite_note-\:16-254]

They were ordered to replace the ageing MiG-21s and [F-6s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shenyang_J-6) then in service,[^cite_note-256] though, due to their low numbers, modernized Chinese J-7s were also ordered later. As of 2022, [Jane's Information Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jane's_Information_Group) reported the [Bangladesh Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Air_Force) (BAF) operated 8 MiG-29s (Six MiG-29Bs & two MiG-29UBs)[^cite_note-257] though they have a low serviceability rate. Four of the eight aircraft in service were overhauled in 2019 with their time before overhaul validity till 2030, with the remaining four to be overhauled in JSC 558 in Belarus, which were to be delivered back in October 2025. Bangladeshi Sources state that six of the 20 delivered jet engines are operational and a deal was proposed by the Airforce in 2025 to overhaul 12 more engines under a \$30.6 Million dollar commitment to be paid over four years with JSC NASC. Previous attempts to overhaul the engines had been hampered by the Russo-Ukrainian War.[^cite_note-258]

### Failed bids

#### Afghanistan

During the [Soviet withdrawal from Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_withdrawal_from_Afghanistan), the [Afghan Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Air_Force) requested the MiG-29 as a premier fighter after receiving 49 helicopters and 125 aircraft in 1989–1990, but the deal was turned down.[^cite_note-259] Soviet MiG-29s were used to provide aircover for crucial sites and Soviet forces based out of Soviet territory following a failed attempt by DRAAF defectors.[^cite_note-\:8-34]

#### Argentina

In 2021 Russia offered the [Argentine Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argentine_Air_Force) a batch of 15 MiG-29 fighters and another of [Su-30](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Su-30) fighters with 12 units and seek also the sale of [Yak-130](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yak-130) training jet and [Mil Mi-17](1493374.md) helicopters.[^cite_note-260] Argentina selected ex-[Danish Air Force](548819.md) F-16As and Bs as part of its new multi-role fighter, with the first BM Block 10 model being delivered to Argentina on the 24th of February, 2025.[^cite_note-261][^cite_note-262][^cite_note-263]

#### Congo (Republic of)

The [Republic of the Congo Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congolese_Air_Force) (*Force Aérienne Congolaise*) historically operated Soviet MiG-21s and sought to replace them with modern aircraft in the early-2000s. The Congo placed an order for five MiG-29SEs (Project 9.13E) and one MiG-29UB with RSK MIG in 2001[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon2006462-264] but these were never delivered, the order presumably being cancelled. Two Ex-[South African Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_Air_Force) [Mirage F.1s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dassault_Mirage_F1) were eventually acquired in 2011 after being refurbished by Paramount Group.[^cite_note-265]

#### Ecuador

[Ecuador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecuadorian_Air_Force) placed an order for the aircraft,[^cite_note-\:4-266] specifically 10 MiG-29S and two MiG-29UB fighters,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon2006462-264] in 1997 and was possibly looking at the upgraded SMT or M models.[^cite_note-\:4-266] Peru, which had gone to war with Ecuador two years prior and lost three aircraft in air-air combat,[^cite_note-267] acquired MiG-29s from Belarus at the same time.

#### Lebanon

In December 2008, Russia moved to expand its military influence in the Middle East when it announced it was giving Lebanon 10 fighter jets, that would have been the most significant upgrade of the [Lebanese Armed Forces](17779.md) since the [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_Civil_War) ended almost two decades before. A Russian defence ministry representative said it was giving secondhand MiG-29s to Lebanon for free. This was to be part of a defence cooperation deal that would have included Lebanese military personnel training in Russia.[^cite_note-268] On 29 February 2010, Russia agreed to change the offer to 10 [Mi-24](384591.md) attack helicopters based on a Lebanese request.[^cite_note-269]

#### Finland

Before the dissolution of the Soviet Union, [Finland](10577.md) had a policy of splitting procurement of armaments between western, eastern and domestic suppliers. The MiG-29 was planned to replace the [Finnish Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Air_Force)'s MiG-21 fighters up to 1988, with test flights having been done.[^cite_note-270] In 1992, Finland ordered 64 F-18C/Ds (57 C models, seven D models), with the F/A-18Cs being locally assembled.[^cite_note-271]

#### Nigeria

[Nigeria](21383.md) started negotiating the purchase of MiG-29s from Russia for the [Nigerian Air Force](538869.md) in 1999 but only signed a contract in 2001 for the purchase of five MiG-29As (9.12B Standard) and a single UB.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon2006462-264] These were undelivered for unknown reasons.

#### Zimbabwe

In 1989, Zimbabwe ordered a squadron's worth of MiG-29s to the USSR. Some [Air Force of Zimbabwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Force_of_Zimbabwe) personnel travelled to Russia for conversion courses, but in 1992 the deal was cancelled, as the geopolitical situation of the region was stabilising.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECooperWeinertHinzLepko2011208–9-272]

## Variants

### Original Soviet variants



A squadron of [Bulgarian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_Air_Force) MiG-29 "Fulcrum-A"



[Bulgarian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_Air_Force) MiG-29UB

MiG-29 (Product 9.12) 'Fulcrum-A'

[^cite_note-MGM-273]

Initial production version for Soviet Air Force; entered service in 1983. NATO reporting name is "Fulcrum-A". Variant possessed the Phazotron N019 Rubin radar, OEPS-29 optical-electronic sighting system and helmet mounted sight.

MiG-29 (Product 9.12A) 'Fulcrum-A'

Export variant of the 9.12 for [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact) countries which included a downgraded RPLK-29E radar, downgraded OEPrNK-29E optoelectronic and navigation systems and older IFF transponders. This variant also lacked the capability to deliver nuclear weapons. Delivered to East Germany, Czechoslovakia, Poland, Sudan, and Romania.

MiG-29 (Product 9.12B) 'Fulcrum-A'

MiG-29 variant of the 9.12 for non Warsaw Pact countries which included a further downgraded radar and avionics. Delivered to India, Iraq, Syria, North Korea, Cuba, Malaysia, Myanmar and Eritrea.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon199944-274]

MiG-29UB (Product 9.51) 'Fulcrum-B'

Twin seat training model. Infrared sensor mounted only, no radar. NATO reporting name is "Fulcrum-B".

MiG-29 (Product 9.13) 'Fulcrum-C'

Update of the initial production version; entered service in 1986. NATO reporting name is "Fulcrum-C". Variant possessed an enlarged dorsal spine to accommodate a larger No.1 fuel tank and the installation of the L-203BE Gardenyia-1 jammer that was lacking on the initial 9.12 version. This enlarged spine earned the 9.13 version and its successors the nickname of "Fatback".

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon200665-12]

MiG-29 (Product 9.13B) 'Fulcrum-C'

Export variant of the 9.13 provided to North Korea in semi-knocked down (SKD) kits and built in [Panghyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panghyon_Airport) between 1991 and 1992. Like the 9.13, it has the Gardenyia-1 jammer but has downgraded avionics and no IFF.

[^cite_note-275]

&#x20;Other sources say the Gardenyia was not delivered to North Korea.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon200669,_487-276]

MiG-29S (Product 9.13S) 'Fulcrum-C'

The MiG-29S was an update of the original 9.13 model retaining the NATO reporting code "Fulcrum-C" and featured flight control system improvements; a total of four new computers provided better stability augmentation and controllability with an increase of 2° in [angle of attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angle_of_attack) (AoA). An improved mechanical-hydraulic [flight control system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft_flight_control_system) allowed for greater control surface deflections. The MiG-29S can carry 1,150 L (250 imp gal; 300 U.S. gal) under wing drop tanks and a centerline tank. The inboard underwing [hardpoints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardpoint) allow a tandem pylon arrangement for carrying a larger payload of 4,000 kg (8,800 lb). Overall maximum gross weight was raised to 20,000 kg (44,000 lb). This version also included new avionics and the new Phazotron N019M radar and [Built-In Test Equipment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Built-in_test_equipment) (BITE) to reduce dependence on ground support equipment. Development of this version was initiated due to multiple systems being compromised to the West by Phazotron engineer [Adolf Tolkachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolf_Tolkachev). This was the final version of the MiG-29 produced before the collapse of the Soviet Union and only limited numbers were produced.

### Upgraded variants based on original airframe

MiG-29S (Product 9.12S)

Post Soviet upgrade for older 9.12 variants incorporating the changes developed for version 9.13S.

MiG-29SD (Product 9.12SD)

Export variant of upgraded 9.12S with downgraded versions of radar and avionics.

MiG-29SE (Product 9.13SE)

Export variant of the 9.13S with slightly downgraded N-019ME radar with multiple target tracking ability and RVV-AE (R-77 missile) compatibility. The first export model MiG-29 with underwing drop tanks; the inner underwing pylons can carry over 500 kg (1,100 lb) bombs in side by side tandem pairs. Its weapons mix includes R-27T1, R-27ER1 and R-27ET1 medium-range missiles. The aircraft can be fitted with active ECM systems, weapons guidance aids, improved built-in check and training systems. The MiG-29SE can simultaneously engage two air targets.

MiG-29SM (Product 9.13M) 'Fulcrum-C'

Similar to the 9.13, but with the ability to carry guided air-to-surface missiles and TV- and [laser-guided bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laser-guided_bomb). NATO reporting code is "Fulcrum-C".

MiG-29SM (SyAF)

For the Syrian Air Force, and based on the MiG-29SM, except the Syrian MiG-29SM uses the 9.12 airframe. RAC MiG developed a special variant for Syria.

[^cite_note-277]



[German Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Air_Force) MiG-29GT

MiG-29G/MiG-29GT

East German MiG-29A / 29UB upgraded to NATO standards, with work done by MiG Aircraft Product Support GmbH (MAPS), a joint venture company form between MiG Moscow Aviation Production Association and [DaimlerChrysler Aerospace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DaimlerChrysler_Aerospace) in 1993.

[^cite_note-278]

MiG-29AS/MiG-29UBS

[Slovak Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slovak_Air_Force) performed an upgrade on their MiG-29A/-29UB for NATO compatibility. Work is done by RAC MiG and Western firms, starting from 2005. The aircraft now has navigation and communications systems from Rockwell Collins, an [IFF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identification_friend_or_foe) system from BAE Systems, new glass cockpit features multi-function LC displays and digital processors and also fitted to be integrate with Western equipment in the future. However, the armaments of the aircraft remain unchanged. 12 out of 21 of the entire MiG-29 fleet were upgraded and had been delivered as of late February 2008.



MiG-29 *Sniper*

MiG-29 *Sniper*

Upgrade planned for the [Romanian Air Force](2106520.md) by [DASA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DaimlerChrysler_Aerospace), [Aerostar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerostar_\(Romanian_company\)) and [Elbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elbit). DASA was responsible for program management, technical support and the test flight program (together with Elbit), Elbit was responsible for developing the avionics package, while Aerostar implemented the upgrades on the aircraft. The first flight occurred on 5 May 2000.

[^cite_note-279]

[^cite_note-280]

&#x20;The upgrades included the installation of a new modular multirole computer based on the [MIL-STD-1553](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MIL-STD-1553)B data bus, upgraded Western avionics, new radio stations, hybrid navigation system composed of an inertial navigation system and coupled with GPS receiver, identification system, two 152 mm × 203 mm (6.0 in × 8.0 in) MFCDs, a Head-Up Display equipped with UFCP front control panel, new [RWR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radar_warning_receiver), new HOTAS and new ADC. The addition of a new radar and the integration of Western weapons while maintaining Russian ones were also expected.

[^cite_note-281]

&#x20;The program halted due to various reasons, along with the retiring of Romanian MiG-29A/UB in 2003, the [Romanian Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_Government) deciding to further invest in the [MiG-21 LanceR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MiG-21_LanceR) program.

[^cite_note-282]



A Russian Air Force MiG-29SMT

MiG-29SMT (Product 9.17 & 9.19R)

The [MiG-29SMT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan_MiG-29M#Variants) is an upgrade of first-generation MiG-29s (9.12 to 9.13) using enhancements on the MiG-29M. Additional fuel tanks in a further enlarged spine provide a maximum internal flight range of 2,100 km (1,300 mi). The cockpit has an enhanced HOTAS design, two 152 mm × 203 mm (6.0 in × 8.0 in) colour liquid crystal MFDs and two smaller monochrome LCDs. The MiG-29A lacked an advanced air-to-ground capability, thus the SMT upgrade adds the upgraded Zhuk-ME radar with air-to-ground radar detection and integrates air-to-ground guided weapons.

[^cite_note-283]

&#x20;It also has upgraded RD-33 ser.3 engines with afterburning thrust rated at 81 kN (18,000 lbf) each. The weapons load was increased to 4,500 kg (9,900 lb) on six underwing and one ventral hardpoints, with similar weapon choices to the MiG-29M. The late series is called MiG-29SMT(R) 9.19(R). It can also accommodate non-Russian origin avionics and weapons.

[^cite_note-284]

[^cite_note-285]



Bangladesh Air Force MiG-29BM

MiG-29BM

The MiG-29BM (probably Belarusian Modernised, possibly Bolyshaya Modernizaciya – large modernization) is an upgrade conducted by the ARZ-558 aircraft repair plant in Baranovichi, Belarus. It is a strike variant of the MiG-29 and the Belarusian counterpart to the Russian MiG-29SMT. It includes improvements to weapons, radar, as well as adding non-retractable air-air refueling ability. They entered service in 2003 and it is estimated, that ten or so were modernized to BM standard.

[^cite_note-286]

&#x20;The [Bangladesh Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Air_Force) upgraded its MiG-29B similar to BM standard.

[^cite_note-287]

MiG-29UBT (Product 9.51T)

SMT standard upgrade for the MiG-29UB. Namely users, Russia,

[^cite_note-288]

&#x20;Algeria and Yemen.

[^cite_note-289]

[^cite_note-290]

MiG-29UPG

The UPG was a modified variant intended to upgrade the MiG-29Bs used by the Indian Air Force. The version is similar to the SMT variant with an imported avionics suite.

[^cite_note-Upgrade-105]

&#x20;It includes [Zhuk-M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhuk_radar#Zhuk-M_\(Export_Designation_Zhuk-ME\)) radar, new avionics, an IFR probe, DRDO D-29 electronic warfare system, and enhanced RD-33 series 3 turbofan engines.

[^cite_note-Upgrade-105]

[^cite_note-TOI-104]

&#x20;It made its maiden flight on 4 February 2011.

[^cite_note-291]

[^cite_note-292]

MiG-29SMP / MiG-29UBP

The SMP/UBP are upgrades for the Peruvian Air Force MiG-29SE fleet. In August 2008 a contract of US\$106 million was signed with [RAC MiG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan) for this custom SM upgrade of an initial batch of eight MiG-29, with a provision for upgrading all of Peru's MiG-29s.

[^cite_note-mindef.gob.pe-293]

&#x20;The single-seat version is designated SMP, whereas the twin-seat version is designated UBP. It features an improved ECM suite, avionics, sensors, pilot interface, and a MIL-STD-1553 databus. The interfaces include improved IRST capabilities for enhanced passive detection and tracking as well as better off-boresight launch capabilities, one MFCD and HOTAS.

[^cite_note-294]

&#x20;The N019M1 radar, a heavily modified and upgraded digital version of the N019 radar, replaces the standard N010 Zhuk-M of the MiG-29SMT. The upgrade also includes a structural life-extension program (SLEP), overhauled and upgraded engines, and the addition of an in-flight refuelling probe.

[^cite_note-295]

MiG-29N/NUB

Designation of the export of 16 MiG-29SD and 2 MiG-29UB for the [Royal Malaysian Air Force](1137609.md).

[^cite_note-YJJ-296]

[^cite_note-297]

#### MiG-29SM+

The MiG-29SM+ is a significantly improved version of the Serbian airforce MiG-29SE/SM fighter jet. It combines Russian N019M1 radar, French IFF systems, and Chinese precision weapons such as the [CM-400AKG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CM-400AKG), [LS-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LS_PGB) and Russian [KH-29TE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kh-29).[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299][^cite_note-300]

MiG-29MU1

A Ukrainian modernization of the MiG-29A/S.

[^cite_note-301]

MiG-29MU2

A further Ukrainian modernization of the MiG-29S, focused on air to ground munitions.

[^cite_note-302]

### Second-generation variants with modified airframe



Russian MiG-29M

Main article: [Mikoyan MiG-29M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan_MiG-29M)

MiG-29M / MiG-33 (Product 9.15)

Advanced multirole variant, with a redesigned airframe, mechanical flight controls replaced by a [fly-by-wire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fly-by-wire) system and powered by enhanced RD-33 ser.3M engines. NATO reporting code is "Fulcrum-E".

MiG-29UBM (Product 9.61)

Two-seat training variant of the MiG-29M. Never built. Effectively continued under the designation 'MiG-29M2'.

MiG-29M2 / MiG-29MRCA

Two-seat version of [MiG-29M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan_MiG-29M). Identical characteristics to MiG-29M, with a slightly reduced ferry range of 1,800 km (1,100 mi).

[^cite_note-MiG-29M/M2_page-303]

&#x20;RAC MiG presented in various air shows, including Fifth China International Aviation and Aerospace Exhibition (CIAAE 2004),

[^cite_note-304]

&#x20;[Aero India 2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aero_India#5th_edition_\(2005\)),

[^cite_note-305]

[^cite_note-306]

&#x20;MAKS 2005.

[^cite_note-307]

&#x20;It was once given designation MiG-29MRCA for marketing purpose and now evolved into the current MiG-35.



Russian MiG-29OVT on display

MiG-29OVT

The aircraft is one of the six pre-built MiG-29Ms before 1991, later received [thrust vectoring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust_vectoring) engine and fly-by-wire technology. It served as a thrust-vectoring engine [testbed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Testbed) and technology demonstrator in various air shows to show future improvement in the MiG-29M. It has identical avionics to the MiG-29M. The only difference in the cockpit layout is an additional switch to turn on vector thrust function. The two RD-133 thrust-vectoring engines, each features unique 3D rotating nozzles which can provide thrust vector deflection in all directions. However, despite its thrust-vectoring, other specifications were not officially emphasized. It is usually used as an aerobatic demonstrator and has been demonstrated along with the MiG-29M2 in various air shows around the world for potential export.

[^cite_note-308]



A [Russian Naval Aviation](5698051.md) MiG-29K (Izdeliye 9.31)



A Russian MiG-29KR being displayed with folded wings

MiG-29K (Product 9.31)

Main article: [Mikoyan MiG-29K](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan_MiG-29K)

Naval variant based on MiG-29M, the letter "K" stands for "Korabelnogo bazirovaniya" (deck-based). It features folding wings, arrestor gear, and reinforced landing gear. Originally intended for the [*Admiral Kuznetsov* class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuznetsov-class_aircraft_carrier) aircraft carriers, it had received series production approval from the [Russian Ministry of Defence](7875884.md) but was grounded in 1992 due to shift in military doctrine and financial difficulties.

[^cite_note-Rac-309]

&#x20;The MiG Corporation restarted the program in 1999. On 20 January 2004, the [Indian Navy](604522.md) signed a contract of 12 single-seat MiG-29K and four two-seat MiG-29KUB.

[^cite_note-Rac-309]

&#x20;Modifications were made for the Indian Navy requirement. Production MiG-29K and MiG-29KUB share a two-seater size canopy. The MiG-29K has radar absorbing coatings to reduce [radar signature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radar_cross-section). Cockpit displays consist of wide [HUD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head-up_display) and three (seven on MiG-29KUB) colour LCD MFDs with a [Topsight E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topsight_E) helmet-mounted targeting system. It has a full range of weapons compatible with the MiG-29M and MiG-29SMT.

[^cite_note-310]

&#x20;NATO reporting code is "Fulcrum-D".



An Indian single-seat MiG-29K on [INS Vikrant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INS_Vikrant_\(2013\))

MiG-29KUB (Product 9.47)

Identical characteristic to the MiG-29K but with tandem twin seat configuration. The design is to serve as trainer for MiG-29K pilot and is full combat capable. The first MiG-29KUB developed for the Indian Navy made its maiden flight at the Russian Zhukovsky aircraft test centre on 22 January 2007. NATO reporting code is "Fulcrum-D".



A [Russian Air Force](1526308.md) MiG-35UB

MiG-35 (Product 9.41SR)

Main article: [Mikoyan MiG-35](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan_MiG-35)

A development of the MiG-29M/M2 and MiG-29K/KUB. NATO reporting code is "Fulcrum-F".

[^cite_note-\:3-311]

MiG-35D (Product 9.47SR)

Dual Seater variant of MiG-35 (see above).

[^cite_note-\:3-311]

MiG-35E (Product 9.61)

Export variant of MiG-35 (see above) for India, one example converted from MiG-29K, converted back.

[^cite_note-\:3-311]

MiG-35 (Product 9.67)

Dual Seat export variant of MiG-35 (see above) for India, one example converted from MiG-29KUB, converted back.

[^cite_note-\:3-311]

- [Romanian Air Force](2106520.md) – 17 MiG-29 9.12A and five MiG-29UB were delivered from 1989,

  [^cite_note-365]

  &#x20;1 MiG-29S received from [Moldova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldovan_Air_Force) in 1992.

  [^cite_note-366]

  &#x20;Retired in 2003.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon2006497-367]

 [Serbia and Montenegro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbia_and_Montenegro)

- [Air Force of Serbia and Montenegro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Force_of_Serbia_and_Montenegro) – Inherited from Yugoslavia, six destroyed in 1999.

  [^cite_note-ACIG_380-132]



Slovak MiG-29 in 1999

 [Slovakia](26830.md)

- [Slovak Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slovak_Air_Force) – Slovakia operated in total 24 MiG-29s. 9 MiG-29 9.12As and 1 MiG-29UB were inherited from Czechoslovakia. From 1993 to 1995 Slovakia ordered 12 additional MiG-29 9.12A and 2 MiG-29UB fighters as compensation for Russian debt.

  [^cite_note-368]

  &#x20;12 aircraft were upgraded by the [Russian Aircraft Corporation MiG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Aircraft_Corporation_MiG) and Western companies in 2007 and 2008 to fulfill the [NATO](21133.md) requirements,

  [^cite_note-369]

  [^cite_note-370]

  &#x20;and were maintained by Russian military technicians at [Sliač Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slia%C4%8D_Air_Base).

  [^cite_note-371]

  &#x20;They were officially withdrawn from service on 31 August 2022.

  [^cite_note-372]

  &#x20;Slovakia's Foreign Minister [Rastislav Káčer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rastislav_K%C3%A1%C4%8Der) has said that his country is prepared to transfer their fleet of MiG-29s to Ukraine. He said: "We have not yet handed \[Ukraine] the MiG-29s. But we are ready to do it. We are talking with our NATO partners about how to do it," and such a package would involve "several thousand" missiles.

  [^cite_note-373]

  [^cite_note-374]

  &#x20;Slovakia transferred the first four of its MiG-29 fighter jets, from 13 to be sent to Ukraine on 24 March 2023.

  [^cite_note-375]

 [South Yemen](718749.md)

- [Democratic Republic of Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Yemen) – Received between 6 and 12 MiG-29s from either Moldova and Russia, or Moldova only, in 1994.

  [^cite_note-376]

 [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union)

- [Soviet Air Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Air_Forces) – Passed on to successor states.

 [Ba'athist Syria](57134800.md)

- [Syrian Arab Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Arab_Air_Force) − Had 20 MiG-29s in service with 12 more on order as of January 2017.

  [^cite_note-WAF2017-342]

  &#x20;29 available as of 2023.

  [^cite_note-World_Air_Forces_p.30-343]

  &#x20;After the [fall of the Assad regime](78548066.md) in 2024, the Israeli Air Force conducted airstrikes destroying the remaining assets to prevent them from falling into the rebels hands.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoyle20254−5-377]

 [Syria](7515849.md)

- [Syrian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Air_Force) – After the [Fall of the Assad regime](78548066.md) in 2024, Israeli airstrikes destroyed the remaining aircraft.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoyle20254−5-377]

  [^cite_note-378]

 [Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Federal_Republic_of_Yugoslavia)

- [Yugoslav Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslav_Air_Force) – 14 MiG-29 and 2 MiG-29UB, passed on to Serbia and Montenegro.

  [^cite_note-ACIG_380-132]

## Preserved aircraft

### Aircraft on display

Czech Republic

- On display at the [Prague Aviation Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prague_Aviation_Museum) in [Prague](23844.md).

  [^cite_note-379]

Canada

- One MiG-29UB cockpit on display at [Jet Aircraft Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_Aircraft_Museum) in [London, Ontario](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London,_Ontario), painted in Ukrainian colours.

Germany

- 29+03 – MiG-29G on display at the [Luftwaffenmuseum der Bundeswehr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luftwaffenmuseum_der_Bundeswehr) in [Berlin](3354.md).

  [^cite_note-Bundeswehr-163]

  &#x20;This airframe is the only remaining German MiG-29 in Germany. It was previously on display in Laage before being moved to the Luftwaffenmuseum der Bundeswehr in 2006 as part of the exhibition "50 Jahre Luftwaffe".

  [^cite_note-380]

  [^cite_note-381]

Hungary

- One MiG-29 is on display with other older MiG planes and helicopters at The RepTár Museum of Szolnok, Hungary.

  [^cite_note-382]

  [^cite_note-384]

India

- KB-732 – On display as a gate guardian at [Ojhar Air Force Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ozar_Airport#Ojhar_Air_Force_Station) in [Nasik, Maharashtra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nashik).

  [^cite_note-WarbirdsofIndia-385]

- KB-741 – On display at the Technical Type Training (TETTRA) School in [Pune](164634.md), Maharashtra.

  [^cite_note-WarbirdsofIndia-385]

Latvia

- 9-52 – MiG-29UB on display at the [Riga Aviation Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riga_Aviation_Museum) in [Riga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riga). This airframe is the second MiG-29UB prototype. After 213 test flights around Moscow between 23 August 1982 and 10 April 1986, it was disassembled and parts of the wings and tails were re-used in prototype (9–16). The remains were shipped to [Riga Military Aviation Engineers High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riga_Civil_Aviation_Engineers_Institute), and later handed over to the Riga Aviation Museum in 1994, where it is currently displayed.

  [^cite_note-386]

  &#x20;The remains of this prototype is in a very bad condition, with open fuselage panels and a partly broken canopy.

Malaysia

- M43-06 – MiG-29N on display as gate guardian at [Al-Sultan Abdullah Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Forces_Command_\(Malaysia\)) in [Kuantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuantan).

  [^cite_note-387]

  [^cite_note-mig29putrajaya-388]

- M43-14 – MiG-29N on display as a monument in Dataran Pahlawan complex, [Putrajaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Putrajaya).

  [^cite_note-mig29putrajaya-388]

Poland

- MiG-29G on display at the [Muzeum Wojska Polskiego](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muzeum_Wojska_Polskiego) in [Warsaw](32908.md).

  [^cite_note-389]

- MiG-29GT on display at the [Polish Aviation Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Aviation_Museum) in [Kraków](16815.md). This aircraft was sold by Germany to Poland in 2002 and briefly served in the [Polish Air Force](602835.md).

  [^cite_note-390]

### Airworthy

- N29UB – MiG-29UB owned by [Jared Isaacman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jared_Isaacman).

  [^cite_note-faar20210915-406]

  &#x20;It was previously owned by the [Flying Heritage Collection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flying_Heritage_Collection) in [Everett, Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everett,_Washington). The aircraft was obtained from Ukraine in early 2009. The aircraft has an FAA approved maintenance program and is flyable.

  [^cite_note-fhdc20140514-407]

- N129XX

  [^cite_note-408]

  &#x20;– MiG-29UB owned by RAVN Aerospace (formerly Air USA) and located at the [Quincy Regional Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quincy_Regional_Airport) in [Quincy, Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quincy,_Illinois). This aircraft was purchased by Don Kirlin from Kyrgyzstan.

  [^cite_note-Hoffman-409]

  &#x20;It is available for contract training and flight testing.

  [^cite_note-Aircraft-410]

- Two MiG-29UBs in flying condition were offered for sale from Eastern Europe in spring 2009. These aircraft come from the same source as the flyable aircraft (N129UB) previously owned by the Historic Flight Foundation

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay200912–13-411]

  [^cite_note-fhdc20140514-407]

  &#x20;and now owned by Jared Isaacman.

  [^cite_note-faar20210915-406]

## Specifications (MiG-29)



3-view line drawing of the Mikoyan MiG-29

*Data from* Mikoyan,[^cite_note-RAC_MiG_specs-412] Airforce technology,[^cite_note-airforce-technology.com-24] Deagel,[^cite_note-413] Business World[^cite_note-Astra_missile-414]

**General characteristics**

- **Crew:** 1

- **Length:** 17.32 m (56 ft 10 in)

- **Wingspan:** 11.36 m (37 ft 3 in)

- **Height:** 4.73 m (15 ft 6 in)

- **Wing area:** 38 m

  2

  &#x20;(410 sq ft)

- **Empty weight:** 11,000 kg (24,251 lb)

- **Gross weight:** 14,900 kg (32,849 lb)

- **Max takeoff weight:** 18,000 kg (39,683 lb)

- **Fuel capacity:** 3,500 kg (7,716 lb) internal

- **Powerplant:** 2 × [Klimov RD-33](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klimov_RD-33) [afterburning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afterburner) [turbofan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turbofan) engines, 49.42 kN (11,110 lbf) thrust each&#x20;

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon2006335-415]

  &#x20;dry, 81.58 kN (18,340 lbf) with afterburner

**Performance**

- **Maximum speed:** 2,450 km/h (1,520 mph, 1,320 kn) at high altitude

- **Maximum speed:** Mach 2.3+

- **Range:** 1,430 km (890 mi, 770 nmi) with maximum internal fuel

  [^cite_note-JAU_MiG-29-416]

- **Combat range:** 700–900 km (430–560 mi, 380–490 nmi) with 2 x R-27s, 4 x R-73s at high altitude

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon200666,_377-417]

- **Ferry range:** 2,100 km (1,300 mi, 1,100 nmi) with 1× drop tank

- **Service ceiling:** 18,000 m (59,000 ft)

- **g limits:** +9

- **Rate of climb:** 330 m/s (65,000 ft/min)&#x20;

  [^cite_note-418]

- **Wing loading:** 403 kg/m

  2

  &#x20;(83 lb/sq ft)

- **[Thrust/weight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust-to-weight_ratio):** 1.09

**Armament**\


- **Guns:** 1 × [30 mm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30_mm_caliber) [Gryazev-Shipunov GSh-30-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gryazev-Shipunov_GSh-30-1) [autocannon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocannon). Originally held 150 rounds, reduced to 100 on later variants.

- **Hardpoints:** 7 × hardpoints (6 × underwing, 1 × fuselage) with a capacity of up to 4,000 kg (8,800 lb) of stores, with provisions to carry combinations of:&#x20;

  - **Rockets:** \
    &#x20;

    - [S-5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-5_rocket)

    - [S-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-8_rocket)

    - [S-24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-24_rocket)

  - **Missiles:** \
    &#x20;

    - 2-4 × [R-27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-27_\(air-to-air_missile\))R/ER/T/ET/P/EP [air-to-air missiles](320568.md)

    - 6 × [R-60](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-60_\(missile\))/[R-60M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-60_\(missile\)) AAMs

    - 6 × [R-73](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-73_\(missile\)) AAMs (later variants also carry the R-73M, R-74 and R-74M)

    - 4 × [Astra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astra_\(missile\)) ([Indian Air Force](403355.md))

    - [Rampage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rampage_\(missile\)) ([Indian Air Force](403355.md))

      [^cite_note-419]

    - [AGM-88 HARM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AGM-88_HARM) (Integration by [Ukrainian Air Force](1135769.md) during the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md))

      [^cite_note-420]

      [^cite_note-421]

    - 2 × [Kh-29](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kh-29)T/TE (MiG-29SMT, MiG-29M/M2, MiG-29K/KR and MiG-35 only)

    - 6 × [R-77](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-77_\(missile\)) (MiG-29S, MiG-29M/M2 & MiG-29K only)

    - 2 × [CM-400](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CM-400) ([Serbian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Air_Force_and_Air_Defence) MiG-29SM)

      [^cite_note-SMAAWC-151]

  - **Bombs:** \
    &#x20;

    - 6 × 665 kg (1,466 lb) bombs

    - 4 × [FAB-500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FAB-500)-M62 500kg general purpose bomb

    - 4 × [FAB-250](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FAB-250)-M62 250kg general purpose bomb

    - 4 × [BETAB-500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BETAB-500) 500kg concrete piercing bomb

    - 4 × [KMGU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KMGU)-AO2/PTAB munitions dispenser

    - 4 × [RBK-500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RBK-500)-PTAB/255-PTAB 500kg cluster bomb

    - 4 × [RBK-250](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RBK-250)-PTAB/275-AO 250kg cluster bomb

    - 4 x [LS-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LS_PGB) precision-guided bomb kit ([Serbian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Air_Force_and_Air_Defence) MiG-29SM)

      [^cite_note-SMAWC-150]

    - [Smart Anti-Airfield Weapon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smart_Anti-Airfield_Weapon) (Indian MiG-29s)

      [^cite_note-422]

  - **Other:** \
    &#x20;

    - 1 × 1400 L external [drop tank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drop_tank)

    - 2 × 1100 L external [drop tank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drop_tank)

**Avionics**\


- Radar depands in variants: N019 family [radar](25676.md) or Phazotron [Zhuk-M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhuk_\(radar\)#Zhuk-M_\(export_Designation_Zhuk-ME\)) (export designation Zhuk-ME) radar

- OEPS-29 [IRST](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrared_search_and_track)

  [^cite_note-423]

- SPO-15 'Beryoza' [RWR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radar_warning_receiver)

  [^cite_note-424]

- [DARE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defence_Avionics_Research_Establishment) D-29 EW system (on [MiG-29 UPG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MiG-29M))

  [^cite_note-DRDO-106]

## Accidents and incidents

|  | This list is [incomplete](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:WikiProject_Lists#Incomplete_lists); you can help by [adding missing items](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Mikoyan_MiG-29\&action=edit). *(January 2025)* |
| --- | --- |

The Indian air force and navy have purchased 80 and 45 aircraft of their respective variants, and have lost 20 and five aircraft from 1989 to 2024, respectively.[^cite_note-425]

- On 23 February 2017, a [Belarusian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belarusian_Air_Force) MiG-29BM crashed. The engine caught fire during takeoff, but the pilot ejected safely at Bobruisk, Mogilev Region.

  [^cite_note-426]

- On 8 May 2020, an IAF MiG-29 from the [223 Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No._223_Squadron_IAF) crashed near [Jalandhar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalandhar) in [Punjab](24751.md) with its pilot ejected safely after the aircraft failed to respond.

  [^cite_note-427]

- On 2 September 2024, a MiG-29 (KBU-3112) of IAF's [47 Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No._47_Squadron_IAF) crashed during a night training mission in an unpopulated area near [Uttarlai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uttarlai) of [Barmer district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barmer_district) in [Rajasthan](26291.md) due to technical reasons, and the pilot ejected safely.

  [^cite_note-428]

  [^cite_note-429]

- On 4 November 2024, a MiG-29 (KBU-711) of the [28 Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No._28_Squadron_IAF) crashed en route from [Adampur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adampur_Air_Force_Base) to [Agra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agra_Air_Force_Station) during a routine training mission due to technical issues. The aircraft [stalled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stall_\(aircraft\)) and eventually entered a [flat spin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flat_spin_\(aviation\)) before the crash, with the pilot ejecting safely.

  [^cite_note-430]

  [^cite_note-431]

  [^cite_note-432]
